   - **Vector** (kept from revision 3; computed 2026-11-20 with coreutils `sha256sum` and OpenSSL 4.0.3, reproduced 2026-11-21): token bytes `00 01 … 1f`; input (53 bytes) `6c6973612d656e726f6c6c2d746f6b656e2d7631` `00` `000102…1f`; SHA-256 `e74f49bf65f5c648421c64994582a9b22ef0f1ab253fefaceb1241f83fe313db`; payload text `AAECAwQFBgcICQoLDA0ODxAREhMUFRYXGBkaGxwdHh8`.
2. **Pairing text.** Shown once on the owner's administrative terminal; the owner's custody of it is what authenticates it. `lisa-pair:3:` followed by base64url of: format version; Core URL (scheme, host, port); trust mode `pin` or `ca`; the leaf SPKI SHA-256 (both modes); in `ca` mode the SHA-256 of the owner's CA certificate; the fingerprint of Core's current signing key; dataset ID; enrollment ID; token; expiry; a 4-byte checksum. Typing errors are pinpointed by the checksum.
3. **Getting the CA certificate (`ca` mode).** A fingerprint cannot supply a certificate the server doesn't send, and TLS servers normally omit the root. So:
   1. The device opens TLS to Core and accepts this first connection only if the leaf SPKI equals the payload's pin and the leaf names the intended host. This authenticates Core for the bootstrap.
   2. Over that connection, without the token, it fetches `GET /v2/trust/tls-anchors`: the CA certificate and every intermediate Core is configured with, signed by Core's signing key.
   3. It checks the CA certificate's SHA-256 against the payload and the bundle signature against the payload's key fingerprint, then evaluates the leaf with the server's chain plus the bundle's intermediates, the CA as the only anchor (`SecTrustSetAnchorCertificates` + `…AnchorCertificatesOnly`), and the SSL policy for the intended host.
   4. Only if all of this passes does it send the enrollment request carrying the token.
   - **Afterwards, every connection to Core** requires, in both modes: the intended host name; the leaf's SPKI equal to the current or the next authorized pin (`tlsPins`, §8.1.4); and, in `ca` mode, a chain to the stored owner CA as the only anchor. `pin` mode makes the pinned leaf the only anchor after its SPKI matches, then applies the host policy. A renewal that keeps the leaf key needs nothing more. A **newly keyed leaf** is accepted only after the roster has announced its SPKI as the next pin and the overlap has begun (§8.1.4), or through the recovery path: a peer's newer roster, or re-pairing. This is the owner's trust policy from the revision 4 brief. Trusting any leaf from the owner's CA without a pin (CA-only renewal) would change that policy and is not part of this plan.
   - **Supported Core configurations, enforced at startup and by `lisa core doctor --tls`:** a self-signed or CA-issued leaf in `pin` mode; in `ca` mode, a leaf signed directly by the root or through intermediates, sending the leaf alone, the leaf with intermediates, or the full chain. Doctor verifies that the configured leaf chains to the configured CA through the configured intermediates, that the SAN contains the configured host name, that validity stays within Apple's limit for owner-trusted CAs (825 days, as R1's evaluator documents), and that the anchors bundle matches. Startup refuses any other configuration.
   - This builds on R1's `PinnedServerTrustEvaluator`, which already anchors only to owner certificates and checks host names. R2 pins the SPKI, not the whole certificate, and adds the bundle step.
4. **Device preparation.** The device generates its keys and credential, then builds and signs the request: enrollment ID, token, dataset ID, trust mode and pins, device details, public keys (and, for Design B, the certificate), credential verifier, incarnation-chain start and, for an R1 companion, the legacy link of §8.14.5. **The exact request bytes contain the token, so they are secret-bearing:** they are kept only in a device-only Keychain item, never in the replica, logs, diagnostics or an export. The item expires at the payload expiry plus 24 hours. It is deleted on success, on `enrollment_consumed`, `enrollment_expired` or `enrollment_invalidated`, and at expiry. Diagnostics show only "enrollment retry pending until …".
5. **Core verification,** in one write transaction:
   1. unknown enrollment ID: `enrollment_unknown`;
   2. **consumed with the same request digest: return the stored response (`duplicate`), whatever the expiry.** This check comes before the open-enrollment checks;
   3. consumed with another digest: `enrollment_consumed` (409) and a security event;
   4. invalidated or expired: the typed error;
   5. token hash compared in constant time; a mismatch commits `attempts + 1`, and the fifth invalidates;
   6. signature, keys, fields, the 10-device limit, and the legacy link if present; a failure commits `attempts + 1` without consuming;
   7. consume: record state and digest; create the device, keys and credential verifier; process the legacy link; sign a new roster and the authorization record; store the public-only response for 7 days. Commit, then answer.
   - **Retention windows, reconciled.** The device keeps its retry item until the payload expiry plus 24 hours, so a legitimate retry arrives at most about 24 hours and 10 minutes after the token was issued. Core keeps the stored response for 7 days after consumption, which is always longer. After that purge, Core keeps the enrollment's compact terminal record (state, digest, device ID, time) for the life of the dataset. A request with the same digest then gets the terminal error `enrollment_response_purged` (409), naming the device ID, and never a second consumption. Only a copied or replayed request can arrive that late; the device's screen says "This pairing finished earlier. Pair again if this device isn't connected."
6. **Completion.** The device verifies Core's signature against the payload fingerprint, stores the results, and deletes the retry item.
7. **Design A's discovery secret** is fetched separately and never travels in the enrollment response (§8.7).
8. **Pairing on Apple devices.**
   - Text entry is complete on every device and is the first device's only path.
   - An enrolled Mac can show the owner's text as a QR code with CoreImage's `CIQRCodeGenerator`.
   - An iPhone or iPad can scan it with VisionKit's `DataScannerViewController` when `isSupported` and `isAvailable` are true. The Info.plist gets `NSCameraUsageDescription`: "Lisa uses the camera only to read the pairing code shown by your Lisa Core or another of your devices."
   - If the camera is denied, restricted or unsupported, the scan button explains why, offers Settings, and leaves the text field ready. Nothing else changes.
   - Core's CLI renders no QR code, because no QR encoder is approved. That missing dependency is recorded and not requested.

**After a failure or retry**
- **Lost response, crash or restart:** the identical bytes are resent and get the stored response.
- **Concurrent requests:** they serialize. The first consumes the enrollment; a different digest gets 409; an identical one gets the stored response.
- **Trust failure:** wrong pin, wrong anchor, wrong host, a CA hash mismatch or a bad bundle signature stops the attempt before the token is sent. No enrollment POST is made and no token byte leaves the device. In `ca` mode, the token-free `GET /v2/trust/tls-anchors` may already have happened when the bundle check fails. The enrollment expires unused.
- **Expiry and invalidation:** a typed error. `lisa core device enroll --list` and `--cancel` manage open enrollments.
- **Never-completed device:** a device that never received its response appears as "enrolled, never seen" for the owner to revoke.

**Acceptance**
- **M1:** LisaTrust will reproduce the vector.
- **M3 (S-04, S-05):** a harness will run against the real TLS server with a test-only libcurl transport that implements the same evaluation (§8.6.5), using CAs and certificates made at run time. It will check:
  - `pin` mode with a self-signed and a CA-issued leaf;
  - `ca` mode with the root omitted, a direct-root leaf, a leaf behind an intermediate the server omits (supplied from the bundle), a renewal that keeps the leaf key, and a newly keyed leaf from the same CA: refused while its SPKI isn't an authorized pin, accepted once the roster announces it as the next pin, and accepted through a peer's newer roster;
  - a wrong anchor, wrong host, wrong pin, wrong CA hash and tampered bundle. In each, Core's request log will show **zero enrollment POSTs**, and a capture of the client's outgoing bytes will contain **no token bytes**. A token-free anchors GET may appear before a bundle-check failure;
  - five wrong tokens, a bad signature, concurrent requests, a response dropped after commit and retried after expiry, a retry after the 7-day purge (`enrollment_response_purged`), a restart between send and response, and the 10-device limit;
  - that no replica table, log line, diagnostic output or export contains the token or the retry bytes.
- **X2 (S-04, S-05):** on an iPhone through the production `LisaSyncHTTP` transport and `Apps/Shared/Trust`, against a Core the owner runs with a test CA, the same chain matrix will run. On every trust failure, Core's log will show zero enrollment POSTs and no token disclosure (at most the token-free anchors GET). The Keychain retry item will be seen during the attempt and gone afterwards. Text pairing will work with camera access denied.

#### 8.1.3 Roster, revocation and paged trust evidence

- **Roster:** signed by Core, identified by (epoch, version), and re-signed at least daily while Core runs. It carries the dataset, issue time, policy version, maximum nearby trust age (30 days by default), each device's keys, status and capabilities, the TLS pins (current and next), the export public key, and the anchors-bundle digest.
- **Revocations** carry the position and epoch at which they happened. They are permanent (union rule): no later roster, epoch or restore removes them.
- **Large artifacts are paged and bound as a whole.** A roster with key history, a causal summary or a snapshot manifest may span several pages. A signed header covers artifact type, dataset, epoch, version, page count and the Merkle root of the ordered pages (leaf `SHA-256(0x00 ‖ page)`, node `SHA-256(0x01 ‖ left ‖ right)`). Each page travels with its inclusion proof. A receiver accepts the artifact only with every page verified, so a peer can neither drop a revocation page nor reorder pages.
- **Devices** keep the newest verified roster, refuse older versions within an epoch, pass rosters to peers, refuse revoked peers, and stop forwarding or applying their new operations. An expired roster stops new nearby exchanges only.
- **`lisa core device revoke`** raises the roster version, revokes the credential, cancels pending deliveries and assignments, and rejects every new operation from that origin, whatever time it claims. Committed data stays.
- **Documented limit:** a device without the update can't know of a revocation.

#### 8.1.4 Key and credential lifecycle

- **Credential reset:** `lisa core device reset-credential <id>` issues a one-time token (hash label `lisa-reset-token-v1`, 10 minutes). The device presents it with a new verifier, signed by its existing key. Core replaces the verifier atomically, and the old credential stops working.
- **TLS rotation:**
  - The roster carries `tlsPins: [current, next]` with windows; devices accept either during the overlap (30 days by default), and Core switches certificates inside it.
  - In both modes, a renewal that keeps the leaf key needs no pin change; a new leaf key needs the overlap. `lisa core doctor --tls` refuses a configured leaf whose SPKI isn't the current or announced next pin. Replacing the CA itself works the same way with `anchors: [current, next]`.
  - A device that missed the overlap gets the new values from a peer's Core-signed roster, or by re-pairing.
  - Health warns 30 days before expiry, and an expired certificate is never accepted.
- **Signing-key rotation:** the new key is announced in a record signed by the old and the new key, with an activation position. Devices keep the key history, so receipts, batches and rosters verify with the key valid at their position.
  - New records must be signed by the current key; a retired key verifies only records from before its retirement.
  - The transition record is also appended to the recovery ledger (§8.4.4) and included in every later backup set.
- **Export-key rotation:** the roster announces the new key. Archives name the key they were sealed to, and Core keeps retired private keys.
- **Device key rotation:** a key-update operation signed by the old and new key, followed by a new roster.
- **Design B certificates** are renewed at 80% of their lifetime through key updates.
- **Where the current signing key's private half comes from on a fresh host.** A signed K1→K2 transition proves only the public-key history. Core can sign as K2 only with K2's private half, which comes from exactly one of:
  - the secret files the owner restores to the new host (§8.11.2);
  - `lisa core trust import-key --file <key backup>` (offline mode), which accepts a private key only if its public half equals the newest key in the merged key history.

  Core never signs a transition with a retired key, so it never forks the key history from K1 after K2's activation.
- **Losing the current signing key's private half:** Core cannot produce a transition that devices accept. The owner then re-anchors every device with new owner-authenticated pairing text, `lisa-reanchor:1:…`, carrying the new key fingerprint, dataset, current head and the device's ID.
  - The device keeps its identity, keys and journal, verifies the new head against the text, and resumes.
  - This is the only path that replaces trust without a signature chain, and it needs the owner at each device.
- **Owner backups:** `docs/SECURITY.md` lists the secret files the owner must keep offline and restore separately: current and retired signing keys, export keys and the TLS key (§8.11.2).

**Acceptance (M3, X2, X7; S-04, S-10):** the harness will check credential reset (the old credential refused), a pin overlap and switch in both modes, a newly keyed leaf refused before its announcement, a device offline for the whole overlap recovering through a peer's roster, signing-key rotation with historical receipts, K2's private half imported on a fresh host (and a mismatching key refused), an export-key rotation with an old archive still opening, device key rotation, roster renewal, an expired certificate refused, and re-anchoring after the signing key is lost. X2 and X7 will repeat reset and pin overlap with real Keychain items.

#### 8.1.5 Challenges (recovery and other key-proven requests)

**Required:** recovery must authenticate devices whose credential verifiers a restore may have rewound (R2-CORE-06, R2-TRUST-03).

**Mechanism**
- **Issue.** `POST /v2/challenges` returns `{challengeID, nonce (32 bytes), purpose, datasetID, epochID, deviceID, expiresInSeconds: 120}`. Core keeps it with the current run generation and a monotonic deadline.
- **Response.** The device signs `"lisa-challenge-v1" ‖ 0x00 ‖ purpose ‖ datasetID ‖ epochID ‖ challengeID ‖ nonce ‖ deviceID ‖ keyID ‖ SHA-256(request body)` with its operation key. The body is the evidence upload or request that the challenge authorizes, so the signature binds the content too.
- **Verification,** in one transaction:
  - the challenge exists, is unused and belongs to this run generation and epoch;
  - Core's monotonic clock is before the deadline;
  - the key is active for that device in the roster as merged so far, including evidence imported moments ago;
  - the signature verifies.

  The challenge is then marked used.
- **Expiry and clock changes:** expiry uses Core's monotonic clock. A wall-clock step has no effect, and a Core restart invalidates every outstanding challenge, because the run generation differs.
- **Replay:** single use, and bound to purpose, epoch and body digest. A response for epoch E2 is useless in E3, and evidence signed for one upload can't authorize another.
- **Revocation mid-exchange:** if imported evidence revokes the device between issue and response, verification fails. The device's upload is kept as quarantined evidence: listed for review, never admitted.
- **Purposes:** `recovery-evidence`, `trust-evidence` (a device whose key the merged roster knows but that can't accept the restored head, §8.5.3), `credential-refresh` (§8.5.5), `owner-import` (§8.14.4) and `legacy-link-lookup` (§8.14.5).
- **A device whose current key the merged roster doesn't know** — it enrolled, or rotated its key, after the backup — can't answer any challenge. Its trust records reach Core only as an owner-mediated trust bundle, authorized by a one-time code or carried as a file, and verified before anything in it counts (§8.5.3).

**Acceptance (M3, S-10):** the harness will check an expired challenge, a reused challenge, a response for another epoch, a body changed after signing, a Core restart between issue and response, a backward wall-clock step of one hour, a revocation imported between issue and response, and a challenge signed by a key the merged roster doesn't know (refused, with the trust-bundle path offered).

### 8.2 Operations (R2-SYNC-01, -02, -03; causal processing, protocol limits)

#### 8.2.1 Envelope, digest, duplicates and receipts

- **Fields:** protocol and schema versions; dataset ID; origin (device, incarnation, sequence; or Core's legacy-translation origin); operation ID (random 128 bits, stable for the life of the logical action; derived only for Core-signed legacy children, §8.12.2); entity type and ID; operation type and payload; dependencies (operation IDs, at most 32); observed set (§8.3.2); policy version; on Core-signed legacy children only, **`legacyParent {scope, requestID, discriminator, index, count, setDigest}`** (§8.12.2; R1's fingerprint stays in Core's parent and is never replicated); creation time (display and audit only; left out of legacy children, whose every field is fixed by the original request). A device-signed operation that carries `legacyParent` or a derived legacy ID is `rejected: malformed`. A device submits an R1 request only as a **legacy import item** holding the original bytes (§8.14.4), never as an operation.
- **Encoding:** canonical JSON (§8.16.1), strict bounded decoder, no duplicate keys, no unknown envelope fields.
- **Digest:** SHA-256 over the complete canonical signed body. **Signature:** raw 64-byte P-256 ECDSA over `"lisa-r2-op\n"` ‖ body, not part of the digest. Relays forward exact bytes.
- **Duplicates, at Core and on every replica, with no exception (R2-SYNC-02):**
  - same ID, same digest → `duplicate` with the current receipt;
  - same ID, different digest → `rejected: identity_reuse` and a security event;
  - same (device, incarnation, sequence) under another ID → `rejected: sequence_reuse` and a security event.

  Legacy requests reaching Core by two paths (a v1 retry and a queue import) are deduplicated at Core by their parent `(scope, requestID)` before any operation exists (§8.12.2). Their children are a pure function of the original request bytes, so every legitimate copy of a child, in any epoch, has the same digest.
- **Receipts:**
  - Waiting operations get signed provisional receipts (`waiting_for_dependencies`, the missing IDs, no position).
  - Decisions give final receipts: `accepted`, `accepted_with_conflict` or `rejected` with reason and recovery hint, each carrying dataset, epoch, operation ID, digest and commit position (R2-SYNC-03).
  - Every decision is also a change-feed entry. A retry while waiting returns the same provisional receipt.

#### 8.2.2 Dependencies and bounded release (causal processing)

| Situation | Outcome |
|---|---|
| A dependency not present | `waiting_for_dependencies` with a provisional receipt; after 14 days `rejected: dependency_missing` with a recovery hint |
| A dependency rejected | `rejected: dependency_rejected` if the entity never existed; otherwise applied with the rejected version ignored (§8.3.1) |
| A cycle among waiting operations (only forged or corrupt input) | Every member `rejected: dependency_cycle` and a security event. Detected when the cycle closes, by walking the waiting graph from the new operation (bounded by the waiting limits below) |
| More than 32 declared dependencies | `rejected: malformed` |
| Waiting storage | At most 1,000 waiting operations per origin incarnation and 64 MiB of waiting bodies in the dataset; beyond that new submissions get `insufficient_storage`. Waiting operations are never dropped |

**Release in bounded, restartable steps.** One decision can release thousands of waiting dependents, each of which can release thousands more, so neither edge enumeration nor enqueueing happens inside the deciding transaction:
- The deciding transaction records the decision and inserts one **release marker** `(operationID, edgeCursor = start)`. That is constant work, however many dependents exist.
- An **expansion step** takes one marker and reads at most 256 dependency edges after its cursor, from the index `(dependencyID, dependentID)`. For each dependent it re-checks readiness (at most 32 dependencies each) and inserts the ready ones into `ready_queue(operationID, enqueuedAt)`. It then advances the marker's cursor, or deletes the marker after the last edge. Each step is one transaction of at most 256 edges and 256 inserts.
- A **release step** takes at most 256 ready entries, oldest first and round-robin across origins. It applies each as at intake and records its receipt; each decision inserts its own release marker. Nested fan-out therefore becomes more markers, never a larger transaction.
- Expansion and release steps alternate, and intake of new operations interleaves with both, so a capture waits for at most one step.
- **Cycle detection** walks the waiting graph from the arriving operation for at most 1,024 nodes in the intake transaction. A longer walk continues as its own bounded steps, and the arriving operation stays waiting meanwhile.
- A crash loses nothing: markers, cursors and the queue are durable, and re-checking is idempotent.
- Capabilities show the marker count and the queue length.

#### 8.2.3 The operation catalog (operation coverage)

- **Capture:** create (text, link, idea, or shared item; large text as a blob, §8.16.3).
- **Tasks:** create; edit a field (title, priority, deadline or clear, estimate, splittable, confirmation); complete; cancel; reopen (naming the terminal actions it reopens).
- **Notes and tags:** add a note piece; replace observed note pieces (a v1 `notes` text edit, §8.3.2); change tags (observed-remove).
- **Ideas:** create directly, not only through a capture; edit a field (summary, priority, estimate, deadline); change lifecycle (R1's `IdeaLifecycle`).
- **Owner reminders:** create (due time, optional subject, note), edit, cancel. Before Core accepts one, its creating device owns delivery (§8.10).
- **Reminder occurrences:** acknowledge, dismiss, snooze — each names the firing it acts on (§8.10.1).
- **Event proposals:** review, dismiss, correct.
- **Preferences:** set, delete or confirm a permitted key. Administrative keys are refused for devices (R2-TRUST-05) and stay CLI-only.
- **Plan blocks:** accept, lock, reject, defer, complete, move.
- **Plan intents** on a provisional local plan: accept, lock, move or reject a local block, keyed by date, zone, subject and range. Core reconciles them with canonical planning; an intent that no longer fits becomes a conflict and is never rescheduled silently.
- **Suggestions from fallback inference:**
  - propose (model authority, from a device);
  - accept or dismiss (owner).

  Accepting creates the task, idea or proposal through ordinary owner operations, with the suggestion as provenance.
- **Any owner item:** delete (tombstone); restore as a new identity with provenance.
- **Conflicts:** resolve, as one operation or as a paged resolution group (§8.3.2).
- **Devices:** key update.
- **Core-originated:** source, model and system writes through `AuthorityWriter` (§8.3.5); legacy children, the one conversion of an R1 request (§8.12.2).
- **Legacy import item** (not an operation): a device's upload of an original R1 queue entry for Core to classify and, with authority, convert (§8.14.4).

`docs/SYNC-PROTOCOL.md` specifies each type with test vectors (valid, tampered, duplicate-key, identity-reuse, a legacy child with the same ID and a different digest refused, a device operation carrying `legacyParent` refused, oversized).

#### 8.2.4 What compacted evidence proves

After an operation body is compacted (§8.4.5), Core keeps for the life of the dataset: operation ID, digest, origin (device, incarnation, sequence), `legacyParent` if any, outcome and reason, epoch and commit position, and the version identifiers (dots) it created. From that:
- **Acceptance or rejection:** a resubmission with the same ID and digest gets `duplicate` with the original outcome; a different digest is still `identity_reuse`. Acceptance and rejection therefore stay provable without the body.
- **Observed versions:** a later operation whose observed set names compacted versions is checked against the retained dots and the Core-signed causal summary (decided ranges per origin), so causal validity stays checkable.
- **Not provable after compaction:** the operation's payload and the exact values it wrote once they are superseded. Replay of a compacted operation after a restore needs its body from another holder (§8.4.5); compacted metadata alone cannot re-apply it.

**Acceptance (M1 codec, M3 Core; S-16):** the harness will have a case per row of the table, including a forged two-operation cycle, the 32-dependency boundary, waiting-storage backpressure, a legacy child with the same ID and a different digest refused at Core and on a replica, and a release of 5,000 dependents interrupted by a process kill after the first batch. **Nested fan-out:** one decision will release 5,000 dependents, each with 20 dependents of its own (100,000 edges), while a capture waits. Every transaction will be measured, and none will enumerate more than 256 edges or insert more than 256 entries. The capture will wait for at most one step, recorded in capture p95. A kill at each step will resume without loss or repetition.

### 8.3 Merge rules (R2-SYNC-06, R2-WORK-03; finding 7; operation and authority coverage)

#### 8.3.1 Rules

Each owner field has a merge class, and each operation names what it observed.

| Situation | Rule |
|---|---|
| Duplicate operation | Applied at most once (§8.2.1) |
| Edits to different fields | Each field is its own register; the edits merge |
| Concurrent edits to one scalar field | Multi-value register: an edit replaces only the versions it observed; every unobserved candidate survives and a conflict opens |
| Owner correction versus a model or source value | Every value carries an authority. A model or source write never replaces an owner value, concurrent or later; it is kept as a suggestion. An owner write replaces what it observed, whatever its authority. A value of ambiguous authority (§8.14.2) is protected like an owner value |
| Concurrent note additions | Separate note pieces; both kept |
| Concurrent tag additions and removals | Observed-remove set |
| Completion or cancellation versus another edit | Status is separate; the edit applies and the terminal state stays |
| Concurrent completion and cancellation | Both recorded; terminal ("completed and cancelled", for review); reminders and deliveries suppressed |
| Reopening | Names the terminal actions it reopens; an unseen one stays in effect |
| Acknowledge or dismiss versus a concurrent snooze of the same firing | That firing is suppressed; the snooze stays in history and creates no new firing (§8.10.1) |
| Concurrent snoozes of the same firing | The latest snooze instant, capped at the policy maximum; one new firing |
| Deletion versus stale or concurrent edits | The tombstone dominates in any order; later edits are recorded as superseded while retention allows; restore creates a new identity |
| Concurrent plans or briefs | Separate derived artifacts; never merged |
| Plan intents versus canonical planning | Core reconciles; a clash with a busy event or a held block becomes a conflict |
| Conflict in a planning field | Planned conservatively, marked uncertain, escalation held (§8.3.4) |
| Resolving a conflict | Names the exact observed set (§8.3.2); a candidate outside it stays as a new conflict |

- **Causal readiness:** an operation waits for its dependencies and is never applied out of causal order or skipped. A device that holds an operation also holds and forwards its dependencies.
- **Convergence:** every rule is a join of sets with a deterministic display order: multi-value registers, observed-remove sets, grow-only piece sets, terminal-action sets with explicit reopen references, the snooze maximum and dominating tombstones. Replicas holding the same operations reach the same state in any order. The harness checks every permutation of a representative set (R2-L11) and the R2 §18.2 scenario.
- **No last-writer-wins and no clock authority.** `docs/CONFLICTS.md` records the rules and the owner's resolution flow.

#### 8.3.2 Bounded pieces, references and exact observed sets (finding 7)

- **Pieces reference the item, never the reverse.** Notes, conflict candidates and tag elements are separate records keyed `(itemID, pieceID)`, each at most one operation in size. The item's own state holds only field registers, and each register is bounded by its value limit (§8.3.3). An item with thousands of notes or candidates therefore never grows its own state or any operation that touches it.
- **Observed sets are bounded.** An operation that replaces or resolves "what I saw" names its observed set as:
  - **a horizon** `(epoch, partition, position P)`, meaning every committed piece of that item and field with commit position ≤ P. The replica can state this only if it has installed that partition through P (§8.4.2). Core resolves it exactly because pieces keep their commit positions;
  - **plus explicit piece IDs** for pieces the device saw that were not committed by P (its own, peers' or relayed pending pieces): at most 256 per operation.
- **Paged resolution groups.** When the explicit list exceeds 256, the device emits a group:
  - pages 1…n, each an operation with up to 256 IDs, a group ID, its index, the total count and the SHA-256 of the complete sorted ID list;
  - each page depends on the previous one, and the final page carries the resolution value.

  Core applies the group atomically when the final page arrives with all earlier pages present and the digest matching. A missing page leaves the group waiting under the ordinary dependency rules. Everything outside the horizon and the list (a later arrival) stays as a new conflict.
- **The exact reviewed set survives a restore and an export.** A horizon names commit positions, and positions belong to one epoch, so a resolution replayed into a new epoch can't evaluate it. Every resolution therefore also carries `reviewedSetDigest`: SHA-256 of the sorted version IDs (origin, incarnation, sequence) of every candidate the owner saw. The device keeps the full sorted list as a signed, Merkle-paged **reviewed-set artifact** (§8.1.3), referenced by that digest and kept as long as the operation body. Core stores the artifacts that accompany resolutions with them, so backups carry them too.
  - **In the epoch where the horizon was stated,** Core evaluates the horizon and checks it against the digest. A mismatch means the device's view differed, and the resolution applies only to the explicit IDs; everything else stays in conflict.
  - **In any other epoch, a resolution applies only with its artifact.** If the artifact isn't present, the resolution **waits** — durably, with a provisional receipt `waiting_for_artifact` naming the digest, under the 14-day dependency rule (§8.2.2) — and shows as "Resolution waiting for the list you reviewed". It applies exactly when the artifact arrives from any holder: the originating device, a peer that relayed it, a protected export (§8.6.6), a backup, or Core's own reconstruction, which is allowed only where the horizon's epoch is still evaluable (in a validated backup of that epoch) and only if the reconstruction reproduces the digest.
  - **Operation-first and artifact-first converge.** Whichever arrives first, the resolution applies once, keyed by its operation ID, to exactly the reviewed candidates; candidates the owner never reviewed — arrivals outside the list — stay in conflict in both orders.
  - **If no holder can supply the artifact** (the originating installation is lost and no export or backup holds it), after the 14 days, or earlier at the owner's choice, the owner decides: apply to the explicitly listed candidates only, or discard. That decision is a new owner operation that names the original; a late artifact afterwards is kept for the record and changes nothing, so nothing applies twice. Replay can therefore leave more conflicts than the original, never fewer.
- **v1 `notes` edits** state "observed at projection revision N", taken from the request; Core maps N to the pieces observed at that revision when it decides the child (§8.12.2).
- **Aggregate limits are advisory.** For example, 1,000 notes per item is checked only when a device creates an operation from its own view, never while merging. Overflow is therefore deterministic, and past the limit the UI asks the owner to archive.

#### 8.3.3 Value sizes derived from the encoded operation

- **One rule:** the canonical body of every operation is at most 64 KiB (65,536 bytes), metadata included.
- **Derivation:** for each operation type, `LisaSyncProtocol.maximumValueBytes(type)` = 65,536 − the encoded size of that type's envelope with every optional field at its maximum (32 dependencies, 256 explicit observed IDs, a `legacyParent` reference on legacy children, a reviewed-set digest, maximum-length IDs and names). It is computed by the codec itself and published in `docs/SYNC-PROTOCOL.md` with the derivation.
- **Measured on the encoded value:** the limit applies to the value's encoded bytes after canonical JSON escaping (UTF-8, `\uXXXX` for control characters), so worst-case escaping is covered. Clients check before saving; Core checks at intake; neither allocates beyond the limit (§8.16).
- **Larger values become blobs:** a note, capture text or description over its limit is stored as a content blob (at most 1 MiB) whose digest and length the operation carries. R1's limits fit: titles ≤ 200 characters, idea summaries ≤ 500; capture text up to 20,000 characters may need a blob.
- **R1 notes have no length limit** (§1.6). A migrated note over 1 MiB becomes a **blob list**: an ordered list of chunk digests (each chunk a blob of at most 1 MiB), the total length, and the SHA-256 of the whole value, carried by the migration record. Concatenating the chunks and checking the whole-value digest reproduces the exact bytes, so migration and reverse migration are lossless (§8.14.2, §8.14.8). Blob lists are created only by migration and by edits of a note that is already one; other new values are limited to one blob. Replicas fetch blob lists chunk by chunk like other blobs.

**Acceptance (S-07)**
- **M2, replica only:** two replicas without Core will check:
  - the largest valid value for a field edit and a note piece, built from the worst-case escaping, accepted, and one byte more refused before allocation;
  - two concurrent maximum-size edits to different fields, and two notes added to an item at the advisory limit, in both arrival orders, giving identical results;
  - a resolution observing 1,000 pending candidates, which becomes a four-page group: applied atomically when complete, waiting while a page is missing, and leaving a later candidate as a new conflict.
- **M3, real Core:** the same cases through Core intake, plus the encoded HTTP and frame boundaries of §8.16. A resolution committed in a superseded epoch will be replayed after a restore with its reviewed-set artifact (exactly the reviewed candidates replaced) and without it (only explicit IDs replaced; the rest left in conflict). A migrated 3 MiB note will round-trip through a blob list and reverse migration byte for byte.

#### 8.3.4 Planning with conflicting values

While a planning field has candidates, the planner uses:
- **deadline:** the earliest;
- **estimate:** the longest;
- **priority:** the highest;
- **splittable:** false, so the work needs one contiguous block;
- **block intents in conflict** (lock against move, reject against accept): the block stays in its current canonical placement, is marked "needs review", and is never moved or dropped automatically.

The plan explains each such choice. Escalation (calls, Time Sensitive notifications) is held until the conflict is resolved. Local and canonical planners apply the same rule through `LisaAgendaInputs`.

#### 8.3.5 The authority path for source, model and system writes

**Required:** models do not overwrite explicit owner corrections (R2-ARCH-04.6); source and model content cannot alter trust or permissions (R2-L34).

**Mechanism:** every Core-originated write to an owner entity is a Core-signed operation applied through `AuthorityWriter.apply(_:authority:in:)`, inside the caller's transaction.

| R1 call site (§1.6) | Authority | Change |
|---|---|---|
| `MailBatchProcessor.commit` inserting invitation proposals, inside `ConnectorRunner.synchronize`'s write, which also advances the cursor | `source(sourceID, evidence)` | LisaIngestion changes: the insert becomes an `AuthorityWriter` call in the same transaction, so the cursor and the proposal operation commit together or not at all |
| `ExtractionService.apply` creating or updating tasks and inserting proposals | `model` | Through `AuthorityWriter`; fields flagged `ownerCorrected` stay owner values |
| `PlanService.recordUnschedulable` setting an idea's unschedulable fields | `system` | Through `AuthorityWriter` |
| `ProposalService.rematch` setting match state | `system` | Through `AuthorityWriter`; an owner's "reports added" stays an owner value |
| `PreferenceService.infer` (tests only in R1) | `model` | Same path; administrative keys refused |
| Calendar reconciliation (occurrences only) | Derived record | Unchanged: derived records sit outside owner merge |

Owner actions (reviews, corrections, edits) are owner operations. Derived records (calendar occurrences, emails, evidence, news) replicate as derived records.

**Acceptance (M3; S-01 shared fixtures):**
- an invitation-created proposal will be corrected by the owner; the email will be reprocessed; the correction will stay effective, and the model's value will be kept as a suggestion;
- a process kill inside the connector transaction will leave neither the cursor advance nor the proposal operation;
- a model attempt to change an administrative preference will be refused.

### 8.4 Core ledger, change feed, snapshots and backups (R2-SYNC-03, -05, -07, R2-CORE-04, -06; findings 8 and 9)

#### 8.4.1 Ledger and intake

- **Schema v5 adds:** dataset, epoch and recovery state; writer- and sender-fence records; operations, dependencies, release markers and the ready queue, merge states, pieces, conflicts, provisional and final receipts; origin progress and incarnation chains; the change log with partition lists and per-row versions; tombstones, which keep the deleted entity's caller-scoped request key and, for captures, the content-free `CaptureDTO` fields (§8.12.2); blobs (their bytes in SQLite, §8.16.3) and reference counts; rosters, key history, enrollments, reset tokens and challenges; replication checkpoints and prune floors, snapshots, the backup catalogue and the replay floor; occurrences, firings, ownership, holds and releases, with each delivery claim's `claimID`; v1 parents with authority, child sets (or an unknown set for outcome-only migrated parents), progress, administrative holds and the reply dependency index; redacted receipts for restricted legacy siblings; legacy import items; quarantined trust bundles and their verification state; reviewed-set artifacts stored with their resolutions; provider connector state (cursors, sync tokens, `needs_authorization`), with tokens kept outside the database (§8.19.1); schedules and the model budget (moved from R1's JSON files).
- **Intake** runs in one write transaction with a savepoint per operation:
  1. bounds, dataset, epoch, schema and recovery state;
  2. origin, signature, digest, identity and sequence reuse (§8.2.1, no exception); `legacyParent` only on Core's legacy origin;
  3. authorization;
  4. dependencies (release is queued, §8.2.2);
  5. merge, projection, pieces, conflicts, tombstones and blob references;
  6. delivery and assignment suppression, serialized as §8.10.3 describes;
  7. change-log entries, the operation record (which is also the idempotency record) and the final receipt;
  8. durable follow-up work, run after commit.
- **Legacy requests** never enter through operation intake. A v1 request or an uploaded legacy import item goes to `LisaLegacyRequests`, which finds or creates the parent `(scope, requestID)` and creates the children in one transaction (§8.12.2).

#### 8.4.2 Change feed and interval coverage (finding 8)

**Required:** never advance a client checkpoint past changes it has not installed (R2-API-03); bounded incremental sync with gap handling (R2-SYNC-05).

**Mechanism**
- **Partitions.**
  - Unwindowed collections (tasks, ideas, captures, preferences, proposals, owner reminders, suggestions) are one partition each.
  - Plans and briefs are partitioned by their own date key, one bucket per date.
  - **Calendar occurrences are partitioned by UTC day.** Bucket n covers `[n × 86,400,000, (n + 1) × 86,400,000)` milliseconds since the epoch. An occurrence belongs to **every** bucket its interval overlaps, by R1's overlap rule (§1.6): a timed event's `[start, end)`; a zero-length event's start; an all-day event's span from the start of its first day to the start of the day after, computed in the floating zone as R1 does. Its membership is therefore a contiguous range `(firstBucket, lastBucket)`: two numbers, however long the event.
  - **Windows and the planning zone.** A replica's window is a span of planning-zone days. It covers every UTC bucket that overlaps those days, so one local day can need two buckets, and DST days are handled by the conversion. A planning-zone change re-buckets nothing. It only changes which buckets the window needs, which the rolling-window rules below handle. Every change to the window, the zone or the working set advances the replica's **coverage generation**.
  - **Change-log entries** list every partition they touch, as explicit IDs or, per windowed collection, a bucket range. A move lists the union of its old and new ranges; a deletion lists the range the record last occupied.
- **Row versions.** Every canonical row on a replica, and every tombstone, keeps `version`: the commit position of the last change it reflects. A change is applied to a row only if its position is greater than the row's version. A tombstone's version therefore blocks every older update, and a row updated through one partition is never set back by an older page arriving through another.
- **Signed change pages.** Each page is at most 512 KiB. Core signs `{dataset, epoch, partitions (an explicit list, or per collection a bucket range), fromPosition, toPosition, entries ascending by position}`.
  - A page proves, for every partition it lists, that its entries are all the entries in `(from, to]` touching that partition. A listed partition with no entries is proved unchanged over the interval. A deletion is an entry, so tombstones are proved like any other change.
  - Every page is self-contained, so no page depends on a page set.
- **Partition cursors.** Each replica keeps one cursor per partition it covers. For a page and a partition p it lists, with cursor c:
  - p is being repaired (§8.4.3): the page is kept, verified but unapplied, as a **deferred page** for p, and applied after the repair publishes. Its other partitions are handled now;
  - `to ≤ c`: the page is ignored for p. Its entries are older than what p has installed, and row versions stop them from restoring older values through any other partition;
  - `from ≤ c < to`: the entries in `(c, to]` touching p are applied, each subject to row versions; the entries in `(from, c]` are trimmed; c becomes `to`;
  - `c < from`: a gap for p. The replica asks Core, or a peer whose coverage includes p, for `(c, …]`, and p's cursor stays at its last proved value.

  A cursor never moves backward, and nothing missing from a page is read as deleted.
- **What a partition's proof says about rows it didn't deliver.** A partition p proved through cursor c_p has delivered, through its own snapshots and pages, every row that was a member of p at c_p. The replica keeps one **membership record** `(p, row, version)` for each row p delivered. A row whose membership range overlaps p, whose version is at most c_p, and which has no membership record in p at that version, is **absent from p**: it doesn't count as busy there, a search scoped to p's days doesn't find it, and no list for those days shows it. A row with a version above c_p, delivered through another partition, counts in p until p's own proof catches up with it. This is what stops an older snapshot of one partition from undoing a newer partition's proof of absence (§8.4.3).
- **Overlapping windows that don't nest.** A replica with window A (days 1–60) relays a page listing days 1–60 to a replica with window B (days 30–90). B advances only days 30–60, wherever its cursors meet the contiguity rule. Days 61–90 are untouched, and nothing missing from the page is read as deleted.
- **Rolling windows.**
  - A day entering the window may need buckets with no cursor. The replica fetches a bucket snapshot for each at a Core cut c and installs it as a partition repair (§8.4.3), then continues with pages from c.
  - A day leaving the window drops the cursors and membership records of buckets no remaining window day needs. A row is dropped only when **no** covered bucket overlaps its membership range. An event spanning days 29–31 therefore stays while day 30 or 31 is covered, and still blocks time there. Dropping is cache eviction; nothing is treated as deleted.
- **Missing pages:** a gap, refetched from the last proved cursor.
- **Compaction:** change-log entries older than the history retention (90 days, R2-SYNC-07) are compacted. A partition whose cursor is below the compaction horizon gets `cursor_expired` for that partition only, and is repaired with a bucket or collection snapshot (§8.4.3).
- **Never backward.** A partition cursor never moves backward within an epoch. A new epoch is entered only through the recovery path with a confirmed head (§8.5.2).

**Acceptance (M3 with Core, M6 over the simulated link; S-08):**
- windows A and B as above, with a deletion on day 40 and no changes on day 45: B will apply the deletion, advance day 45 on the empty proof, advance days 30–60 only, and leave days 61–90 untouched;
- **an event spanning days 29–31:** the window will roll past day 29; the event will stay in the replica, and the local planner will still treat its hours on day 30 as busy. When the event later moves to days 33–34, the entry, listed under its old range too, will reach the replica through day 30's bucket;
- **a page during a repair:** day 31's bucket is repaired from a snapshot at cut 120 while a valid page (120,150] listing days 31 and 32 arrives. Day 32 will advance to 150 at once; for day 31 the page will be deferred and applied after publication, leaving day 31 at 150. No cursor will be set to 120 after reaching 150;
- **absence in both orders:** an event spanning buckets A and B leaves both at 130. (1) A complete snapshot of A at 150 is published, then a delayed snapshot of B at 120 that still contains the event; (2) the reverse. In both orders, before, during and after each publication: the planner will never treat A's hours as busy because of the event; a search scoped to A's days won't find it, while an unscoped search finds it only as B's, labeled "as of" B's cut; an overlay editing the event will stay visible on its own row; and the cursors will be A = 150 and B = 120;
- **readers during a repair:** a reader opening the view before, during and after publication will see either the old verified view or the new one for the repaired days, never a mixture of chunks;
- **crash, epoch and window:** a kill after the first staged chunk, another just before publication and another just after it; a new epoch during a repair (abandoned, then a full snapshot); a window roll during a repair (the dropped day leaves the repair, the rest publishes). Each result will equal an uninterrupted repair or the abandonment §8.4.3 specifies;
- **a stale relayed page:** one signed page for positions (90,120] listing partitions A and B, relayed to a device whose cursor for A is 150 and for B is 100. A will ignore it: A's values written after 120, A's tombstones and A's cursor (150) survive. B will apply only (100,120], trimming (90,100], and move to 120. A row in both partitions, updated at 130 through A, will keep its value from 130;
- a planning-zone change: no re-bucketing; only edge buckets fetched or dropped;
- a missing page refetched from the last proved cursor; a compacted range: per-partition `cursor_expired` and a repair;
- a page whose partition list was altered: refused.

#### 8.4.3 Snapshots, partition repairs and paged artifacts

- **Snapshots** come from one consistent cut on the read connection. They are streamed as length-prefixed records in 512 KiB chunks that records may span; each record carries its row version and its membership range.
- **Manifests** are paged and Merkle-bound (§8.1.3). They carry the dataset, epoch, cut, the partitions covered with their cursors, the causal summary, counts and chunk digests.
- **Two ways to install:**
  - a **full snapshot** (first installation, or a new epoch): every partition at once, by the staged swap of §8.6.3;
  - a **partition repair** (a bucket entering the window, `cursor_expired`, a collection refill): only the partitions the snapshot names, never whole tables.

**Required of a repair:** readers see one verified view of each partition, from before the repair or after it, never a mixture; no covered partition's cursor ever moves backward; and an older snapshot of one partition never undoes a newer partition's proof of absence (R2-API-03, R2-SYNC-05).

**A partition repair, step by step**
1. **Record.** A durable repair record names the snapshot, its cut c, the partitions P, the epoch, the replica's coverage generation (§8.4.2) and each partition's cursor at the start. The cut must be at or after each of those cursors; otherwise the snapshot is refused.
2. **Serialize.** From the record's commit until publication, the partitions in P are **repairing**. A signed page that lists a repairing partition is applied as usual to its other partitions and kept, verified but unapplied, as a **deferred page** for the repairing ones (§8.4.2). No repairing partition's cursor moves during the repair, so the repair can never rewind a cursor that a concurrent page advanced.
3. **Stage, invisibly.** Verified chunks become staged rows tagged with the repair's generation, each chunk in its own short transaction of at most 512 rows. From them, still tagged and invisible to readers, the replica builds the partitions' membership records, and the effective-view rows and search entries of every row whose canonical state would change. Readers keep the last published view. The repairing partitions are marked "Updating — not yet verified", and the planner treats a repairing calendar day like a stale one.
4. **Publish, in one transaction of constant size.** It re-checks the epoch, the coverage generation and every repairing partition's cursor against the record. If all of them match, it flips the published generation of P to the staged one, sets P's cursors to c and clears the repairing mark. Readers resolve each row to its newest version among published generations, and the membership of P to P's published generation, so the flip changes canonical rows, membership, view and search for P together. Rows of other partitions and every overlay are untouched; overlays are evaluated against the new canonical rows from that moment.
5. **Apply the deferred pages** in position order by the ordinary cursor rules (§8.4.2): ignored up to c, trimmed and applied across c, or a gap after it, each in its own transaction.
6. **Clean up** superseded rows and staging in bounded chunks that readers never see.

**When the re-check fails:**
- **The epoch changed:** the repair is abandoned, its staging dropped in bounded chunks, and the new epoch installs by full snapshot (§8.6.3).
- **The coverage changed** (a window roll, a zone change): partitions no longer covered leave P, and the repair publishes the rest; if a partition it needs was added, it restarts with a new record.
- **A cursor moved** (impossible without corruption, because pages are deferred): the repair is abandoned and restarted from a newer cut. No cursor is ever set below its current value.

**Absence across partitions.** Publication writes membership records only for rows the snapshot delivers (§8.4.2). A row that another partition delivered, whose version is at most c and which this snapshot didn't deliver, is absent from P from publication on. Take an event spanning buckets A and B that left both at position 130. If A is published at 150 and a delayed snapshot of B at 120 arrives later, the event comes back with B at version 110: it counts in B, as of 120, and never in A, because A's proof through 150 didn't deliver it. In the reverse order, A's publication makes the same event absent from A. Either way it is never busy, listed or found in A, and B's own pages remove it once B passes 130.

**Recovery facts (partition repair).** *Survives:* the repair record, staged rows, deferred pages and every committed chunk. *Who acts:* the replica, automatically. *Safe after an interruption:* before publication the published view is unchanged, and the repair resumes from its record or is abandoned by the re-check; after publication the new view stands and the deferred pages are applied. *What the owner sees meanwhile:* the affected calendar days or collection marked "Updating — not yet verified", with the last verified view still shown and everything else unchanged.

- **Reader lifetime:** generation holds back WAL checkpoints, so its reader is bounded (§8.15.2). Past the bound the generation aborts and is retried later from a new cut.

#### 8.4.4 Backups: manifests, the recovery ledger, anchors and discovery (finding 9)

**Required:** consistent backups with manifest, canonical records, operations and receipts, tombstones, delivery history, enrollment metadata and non-secret configuration references; secrets documented separately (R2-CORE-06). R1's manifests have no checksums and take their counts from the live database (§1.6); R2 fixes both.

**Mechanism**
- **Backup set.** `lisa core backup` writes `<backup root>/<backupID>/`:
  - `lisa.sqlite`, from SQLite's online backup. **Committed blobs live inside SQLite** (§8.16.3), so the image includes every blob any of its records references;
  - `manifest.json`, signed with the current Core key. It holds:
    - the backup ID, dataset and epoch;
    - the **lineage** from dataset creation to this epoch (epoch records with parents, cuts and confirmations, §8.5.2);
    - the cut position and its commit time, schema and application version;
    - the submission journal's last sequence at the cut, `s_cut`, and the `claimID` of every delivery in `submitting` at the cut (§8.10.6);
    - SHA-256 of every file, and counts taken from the backup itself;
    - the Core key history and roster version at the cut;
    - the configuration file's digest and every secret reference by name or path, never a value;
  - `recovery/`, a copy of the recovery ledger **cut at the same position**, and the journal as a labeled prefix.
- **One cut across the image and the files outside it.**
  - Every ledger record that mirrors a database record carries that record's commit position. It is appended, with `fsync`, after its transaction commits; on startup Core appends any committed record the ledger lacks (the database is authoritative within an epoch).
  - The backup's read transaction fixes the cut c. Its `recovery/` copy keeps exactly the records with position ≤ c, and regenerates from the image any record ≤ c the live ledger was still missing. The image and the copy therefore describe the same cut.
  - The submission journal records provider I/O, which has no database position. The copy includes it through `s_cut` as a **prefix**, which never proves that a later submission didn't happen (§8.10.6).
- **The recovery ledger** lives outside the database at `<data>/recovery/`. It is a set of append-only, hash-chained files whose records Core signs:
  - epoch records and confirmations;
  - trust records: roster versions, key transitions and revocations, including those verified from trust bundles (§8.5.3);
  - fence records, each naming the fenced volume's final run, journal sequence, chain hash and commit position where it binds them (§8.5.1);
  - the submission journal (§8.10.6);
  - the backup index (backup ID, location, lineage head, cut).

  Each record is also in the database. The ledger exists because restoring the database rewinds the database's own catalogue, while the ledger on the data volume does not rewind. `lisa core trust export` and `lisa core backup export --to <dir>` copy ledger and backup sets off-host, which the documentation recommends after every revocation, key rotation and backup.
- **Validation.** `lisa core backup validate <id>` recomputes the digests, runs `integrity_check`, checks that the `recovery/` copy ends at the manifest's cut, verifies the manifest signature, and writes `validation.json` into the backup set.
- **An independent anchor is required to promote or to prune.** A manifest carries its own key history, which can't vouch for itself. An anchor comes from outside the backup: the owner's offline key backup (§8.11.2), passed as `--anchor <key backup dir>`, or the Core signing-key fingerprint the owner reads from an enrolled device's Settings › Lisa Core, passed as `--anchor-fingerprint`. The manifest's key history must chain to it.
  - **Without an anchor a backup can only be inspected.** `backup validate` reports "integrity verified, authenticity unverified", and `backup inspect` shows its cut, counts and lineage. It doesn't enter the restorable set, can't be restored into a new epoch, and counts for no pruning decision.
  - **The one exception is explicit re-anchoring.** `lisa core restore <id> --reanchor` restores an unanchored backup under a **new** Core signing key, records that the backup's authenticity is unverified, and leaves every device untrusting the new head until the owner re-anchors each one with `lisa-reanchor:1:…` text (§8.1.4). Fences and recovery stages apply as usual.
- **Retained restorable set R:** the newest `backup.retainRestorable` (default 2) **anchored and validated** backups on the current head's lineage, plus any the owner pins (`lisa core backup pin <id>`; a pin requires an anchor too). Capabilities publish R as (ID, lineage, cut), signed with the current Core key.
- **Discovery after a restore.** `lisa core restore` and `lisa core backup scan --root <dir>…` rebuild the catalogue by reading every manifest under the backup roots and the off-host locations the owner names, verifying each against the anchored key history and the key transitions in the ledger. A newer backup (B after restoring A) and every trust record (a later revocation, a key transition) are therefore found, even though the restored database never knew them.

#### 8.4.5 Replay coverage and pruning across branches (finding 9)

- **Containment.** A backup X's lineage is a list of segments `(epoch, start, end)`, the last ending at X's cut. An operation committed in epoch E at position p is contained in X if and only if some segment `(E, s, e)` of X has `s < p ≤ e`. This answers containment for divergent branches: an operation committed on an abandoned sibling epoch is in no backup taken on the other branch.
- **Two different things a holder can have** for an operation committed after X's cut:
  - **its decision and effect:** the operation's ID, digest, outcome and resulting projection, which a backup or Core's compacted metadata keeps (§8.2.4). That confirms what was decided, but it can't re-apply the operation in a new epoch;
  - **its exact signed bytes:** the body that replay needs (§8.5.4), held by devices, archives, backups taken before compaction, and Core while it retains the body.
- **What restoring X promises, reported per range in three kinds:**
  1. **Confirmed:** X's own state, and operations whose exact bytes are contained in another anchored, validated backup Core holds.
  2. **Recoverable elsewhere:** operations whose exact bytes a known holder has reported holding: a device (§8.5.3), an archive the owner supplied (§8.6.6), or a fenced writer's exported tail (§8.5.1). A range whose decision is known, for example from a newer backup's compacted metadata, but whose bytes no holder reports, is listed as **"decided; bytes not held"**.
  3. **Unknown:** everything else committed after the cut, including operations whose only reported holder evicted them.

  A device's eviction proves only that **that copy** is gone. It moves the range from "recoverable elsewhere" to "unknown" unless another holder reports it, and never to "lost". Lisa never reports a range as unrecoverable: unreported devices, older backups outside the catalogue and the owner's exports remain possible sources, and the report names them.
- **The per-holder coverage report** (`lisa core backup coverage <id>`, also part of the restore review) lists, for each holder — every backup in R and every pinned backup, Core itself, each device by its last checkpoint, each archive or tail the owner imported — and for each origin, the ranges it holds as exact bytes, as decisions only, as evicted, and as unknown, with the catalogue version it last pruned under and the time of its last report. An offline device is shown with its last report's date, never as current.
- **Pruning rule (Core and every replica).** An operation body may be dropped only when all three hold:
  1. its canonical effect is installed on that replica, so no visible overlay needs it;
  2. it is contained in every backup of the replica's latest catalogue R, learned from the current head no more than 7 days ago (with an older catalogue, nothing is pruned);
  3. no live reference needs a blob it references.
- **Compaction at Core** drops bodies older than the history retention (90 days) once the pruning rule allows it; their decisions stay (§8.2.4). Backups taken afterwards hold those ranges as decisions only, and the coverage report says so.
- **An offline replica** prunes under the catalogue it last learned for at most 7 days, then stops pruning. A backup pinned in the meantime isn't in that catalogue, so the replica's prune floor, reported at its next checkpoint, shows what it may already have dropped relative to the pin.
- **Blob references are counted** across canonical records, retained bodies, pending operations, drafts, conflict candidates, staged installations and exports being assembled. A blob is deleted only at zero, so a blob shared by a prunable edit and a pending edit stays.
- **Cap eviction** on a device (90 days or 64 MiB of committed bodies) may drop a body that meets conditions 1 and 3 but not 2. It never drops a visible overlay or a live blob reference. The evicted range is recorded and reported as "replay coverage reduced on this device for backups older than …".
- **Prune floors.** Each replica reports, in its replication checkpoint, the catalogue version it last pruned under and, per origin, the highest position it pruned. Core keeps these floors.
- **Pinning an older backup later.** If the owner pins a backup O after replicas pruned under a catalogue without O, the pin can't bring those bodies back. `lisa core backup pin` therefore reports, per device, what may already be pruned relative to O, and the replay promise for O is stated in the three kinds of range above, computed from the floors. From the pin on, replicas that learn the new catalogue stop pruning anything O needs.
- **Older cuts:** a backup outside R keeps its own state, plus whatever holders still have; nothing beyond that is promised.

**Acceptance (M3; S-09):**
- **Linear A then B, keeping 2:** pruning will keep everything above A's cut, and restoring A will replay all of it from B and the devices.
- **Keeping 1, with one device's eviction:** the report will show confirmed, recoverable-elsewhere and unknown ranges. The evicted range will be "unknown" only where no other holder reports it, and nothing will be called unrecoverable or lost.
- **Compaction:** operations after A's cut compacted at Core and held by B as decisions only. Restoring A will list them as "decided; bytes not held" except where a device, archive or tail reports the bytes, and replay will use exact bytes only.
- **Pinning an older backup after a replica pruned under an earlier catalogue:** the pin report will list that replica's floor, the promise will match it, and no further pruning will touch what the pinned backup needs.
- **An offline replica with its previous catalogue:** offline for 5 days, it keeps pruning under the old catalogue while the owner pins O; its reconnect checkpoint reports its floor, and the pin report lists it. Offline for 8 days, it prunes nothing.
- **Cap-driven eviction:** a device at its 64 MiB cap evicts bodies meeting conditions 1 and 3; the report moves those ranges to "unknown" unless another holder reports them.
- **Sibling histories:** two restores of A, then work on each. Operations on the abandoned sibling will appear in no backup of the other branch and will be replayed from devices.
- **An offline holder:** a device holding bodies, offline during the restore, will reconnect later and complete the replay.
- **A shared blob:** a blob shared by a prunable and a pending edit will survive pruning and cap eviction.
- **Discovering B after restoring A:** B will be found by the scan and offered as a replay source.
- **A consistent cut:** a backup taken while writes, blob promotions and ledger appends continue will validate: the image, its blobs and its `recovery/` copy end at the same cut. A ledger record missing after a crash will be regenerated in the copy.
- **A fresh host:** validation with the key-backup anchor and with a device-shown fingerprint makes the backup restorable. With no anchor it can only be inspected: `restore` refuses it, it never enters R, and pruning ignores it; `restore --reanchor` proceeds only under a new key, with every device re-anchored.
- **Honest coverage:** for every case above, the per-holder report lists each holder's bytes, decisions-only, evicted and unknown ranges, with offline holders dated, and never calls a range lost.

### 8.5 Restore, recovery and admission (R2-CORE-04, -06, R2-TRUST-03; finding 10; recovery admission and client identity)

**Required:** exactly one active Core writes a dataset; a local lock does not stop a second server running a copy; restore and migration must fence or stop the previous Core (R2-CORE-04). A restore that moves history backward establishes a new epoch; a restored Core starts with outbound side effects suspended (R2-CORE-06). Revoked origins stay rejected (R2-TRUST-03).

**Decision:** single-Core design (O-2); no cluster coordination.

#### 8.5.1 Restore procedure, writer fences and sender fences

**Two different questions, never merged:**
- **Writer fence:** can the previous Core still change the dataset by **any** path? A previous Core that runs can accept stale clients' edits, run its scheduler and connectors (plans, briefs, reminder sync, mail and calendar ingestion), take owner commands from its own terminal, sign receipts with the dataset's key, and restart. Only evidence that it is **stopped, with every restart prevented**, closes this question. Cutting it off from clients doesn't: its scheduler, connectors and local commands keep writing.
- **Sender fence**, per channel (APNs, provider mail or SMTP, Twilio, model provider): can the previous Core still submit to that provider? Revoking or rotating a credential closes this for one channel and says nothing about writes (§8.10.6).

Each previous Core is identified by its data volume. Every volume gets a random **volume ID** at `init`, `restore` or `migrate run`, recorded in the lock file, `health.json`, the recovery ledger and every journal record.

`lisa core restore <backupID>` (offline mode, §8.11.5):
1. **Anchor.** The backup must be anchored (§8.4.4), unless the owner chooses `--reanchor`.
2. **Fence this host.** Under the data-directory lock, the replaced database is renamed aside and fenced (schema marker), so a Core started on it refuses to run. The run generation changes. If the replaced volume holds a submission journal, it gets a `terminal` record. The fence record binds the volume's final run, journal sequence and chain hash, and its final commit position (§8.10.6). The recovery ledger records writer fence `fenced-local` for that volume.
3. **List the other writers.** The restore lists every volume that the recovery ledger, the backup catalogue or a device report names as having served the dataset since the cut, and asks the owner about each one not fenced locally.
4. **Writer-fence states,** one per previous writer:

   | State | Evidence | Established by | Closes the writer fence | Binds the journal's end (§8.10.6) |
   |---|---|---|---|---|
   | `fenced-local` | This host's replaced volume, fenced under its lock | Lisa (step 2) | Yes | Yes |
   | `fenced-on-host` | `lisa core fence --local`, run in offline mode **on the previous writer's own host and volume** after its Core has stopped. Taking the volume's lock proves no process holds it. The schema marker makes every restart refuse the database, whatever starts it (a restart policy, a unit, a person). A `terminal` record closes the journal. The signed fence record names the volume ID, the final run, journal sequence and chain hash, and the final commit position. With `--export-tail <dir>`, the command also writes a signed archive of every operation and owner command committed on that volume after the restored backup's cut, for replay at stage 3. The owner carries both over with `lisa core fence import` | Lisa, on the old volume | Yes | Yes |
   | `owner-attested-stopped` | The owner states, following `docs/OPERATIONS.md` § "Stopping a previous Core", that the container is removed (not only stopped), or the host is powered off and kept off, or the volume is destroyed, **and** that every automatic start is disabled (restart policy, systemd unit, Compose `restart:`, scheduled jobs). The method, time and the owner's text are recorded | The owner (`lisa core fence attest --stopped`) | Yes | No |
   | `unknown` | Anything else | — | No | No |

   **Client isolation is information, not a fence.** `lisa core fence note --client-isolated <volume>` records that the owner removed the previous Core's VPN route. It lowers the risk from stale clients and appears in the review, but the writer fence stays `unknown` until the previous Core is stopped with restart prevented. Rotating provider credentials never changes a writer-fence state.
5. **Rebuild the catalogue** from backup roots and the recovery ledger (§8.4.4).
6. **New epoch.** Create a new epoch record (parent: the backup's epoch and cut), enter recovery mode, and suspend delivery.

**While any writer fence is `unknown`**, the restored Core only collects evidence (stage 1, §8.5.4): challenges, evidence uploads and trust bundles into quarantine, receipt lookups, capabilities, read-only status and the `trust review` display. It confirms no head, refreshes no credential and admits no write from any path, and its connectors, scheduler and outbox stay stopped. A confirmation then would publish a second live head while another writer may still extend the first.

**When the replacement may promote.** Stage 2 opens only when every previous writer is `fenced-local`, `fenced-on-host` or `owner-attested-stopped` (and the other stage-2 conditions hold, §8.5.4). Whatever the previous Core wrote after the cut is then recovered from devices' journals, from its exported tail, or not at all, and the restore review says which (§8.4.5). Derived writes (plans, briefs, reminder schedules) are derived again; connectors read their sources again from the restored cursors, and deduplication stops a second ingestion (§8.19.2).

**What survives outside the rewound database:**
- the recovery ledger on the data volume: lineage, confirmations, trust records, writer- and sender-fence records, the submission journal and the backup index;
- backup sets;
- the secret files;
- off-host exports;
- every device's replica and evidence;
- a previous writer's own volume, and its exported tail, if the owner can reach it.

If the whole data volume is lost, only off-host copies and devices remain, and the review in §8.5.3 shows that.

**Recovery facts (fencing).** *Survives:* fence records, with the final positions they bind, in the ledger and its off-host copies; exported tails; a reachable old volume. *Who acts:* the owner, by stopping the old Core and running the fence on its host, or by attesting under the documented procedure; Lisa verifies signed fence records. *Safe after an interruption:* `restore`, `fence --local` and attestations are idempotent and resume from their ledger records; a `fence --local` interrupted before its marker leaves the volume unfenced and is simply run again. *What the owner sees while a fence is unknown:* "Previous Lisa Core on <host, volume> may still be running and changing data — through its schedule, its connectors or local commands — even if your devices can't reach it. This Lisa Core accepts no changes until you stop it with restart prevented (Operations › Stopping a previous Core) or run `lisa core fence --local` on its host. Evidence collection is open."

#### 8.5.2 Lineage, confirmations and the current head

- **Epoch record:** `{epochID, parentEpochID, parentCut, restoredBackupID, createdAt, coreKeyID}`, signed.
- **Owner confirmation:** `{epochID, confirmationSequence, supersedes: [epoch heads], reviewedEvidenceDigest}`, signed by Core when the owner runs `lisa core trust confirm` (§8.5.4).
  - `confirmationSequence` is one more than the highest sequence in the recovery ledger, in the restored database, and in every device report received during recovery.
  - `supersedes` lists every head known at that moment that isn't an ancestor of the new epoch, including a sibling restore of the same backup.
- **How a client chooses the current head.** It accepts a head H only if H carries an owner confirmation that the client verifies (Core-signed with a key in its key history, or after re-anchoring), that confirmation has the highest sequence the client knows for the dataset, and one of these holds:
  - H descends from the client's head through verified epoch records;
  - H's confirmation supersedes the client's head, directly or through a chain of confirmations.

  **A confirmed active head is required for both moves.** An epoch without a confirmation (recovery stages 0–1) is never accepted as current, whether it descends from the client's head or is a sibling of it. The client deals with such an epoch only through challenge-authenticated evidence uploads or a trust bundle.

  So when two restores of the same backup both carry valid confirmations, the later one names the earlier in `supersedes` and has a higher sequence. A client holding either one can verify which is current.
- **When the supersession can't be shown:** a client whose head is neither an ancestor nor superseded refuses ordinary sync with H. It still authenticates by challenge and reports its head as recovery evidence; Core lists it in `lisa core trust review` as an unknown head. The owner then issues a supplementary confirmation that supersedes it, and the client accepts that.

  A tie can't be resolved automatically, because devices never pick between confirmed heads by time.
- **The confirmation sequence also orders notification commands** across epochs (§8.10.2), so a supplementary confirmation outranks every command issued before it.

#### 8.5.3 Trust evidence and key history after a restore

- **Collected before confirmation, by challenge (§8.1.5):** each device uploads its newest roster, epoch records and confirmations, key-transition records, its assignment inventory and observations (§8.10.2), and an inventory of the operations it retains: IDs, digests, origin positions and receipts, not bodies.
  - Bodies may also be uploaded into a quarantined staging area.
  - Nothing is applied before confirmation.
- **Merging:** revocations are a union and are kept. Key transitions are verified as a chain from the anchored key history: K1→K2 signed by K1 and K2.
  - A revocation signed by K2 after the backup is verified through that chain and applied before any work is admitted.
  - Devices enrolled after the backup are re-registered as "membership unconfirmed" until a verified roster names them.
  - `lisa core trust import` adds the recovery ledger's trust records and off-host exports.
- **Core's own key.** A recovered K1→K2 transition proves only the public-key history. Core signs as K2 only after obtaining K2's private half from the restored secret files or `lisa core trust import-key` (§8.1.4). Until then it signs no roster, confirmation or receipt. If K2's private half can't be found, devices that already trust K2 refuse K1-signed records after K2's activation, Core never signs with K1 again, and re-anchoring every device (§8.1.4) is the only way forward.
- **A device that alone holds newer trust evidence, and whose key Core knows.** Suppose only the iPad holds the K1→K2 record and K2-signed rosters (with a revocation made after the backup), because the ledger copy that reached the new host predates them. The iPad refuses the restored head, which can be signed only with K1. It then offers a bounded, **evidence-only** exchange, which the owner starts on the device: "Lisa Core was restored from an older backup and can't yet prove it holds your newest key. Send this device's trust records to Lisa Core?"
  - It authenticates with a `trust-evidence` challenge (§8.1.5) and uploads only signed trust artifacts, within the bundle bounds below. It accepts nothing from the restored Core in that exchange.
  - Core verifies the artifacts as below, merges the revocations before any admission, and shows them in `trust review`.
  - The owner then imports K2's private half, after which the iPad accepts the confirmed K2-signed head, or re-anchors.
- **A device whose key Core doesn't know.** A device that enrolled after the backup, or rotated its key after it, can't answer any challenge, because its current key isn't in the merged roster. Its trust records reach Core as an **owner-mediated trust bundle**:
