Lisa therefore releases a held delivery automatically only on positive evidence from the old sender, bound to its final journal entry by a writer fence, or from the device that may hold the slot. Otherwise it holds the delivery until the owner decides.

**Mechanism**
- **The submission journal** (recovery ledger, one per Core volume, hash-chained). Record kinds:
  - `run-start {volumeID, runGeneration, epoch, previousRunLastSeq, previousRunClosed}`, signed with the Core key;
  - `submit-start {seq, claimID, slot (occurrence, firing, recipient), deliveryID, channel, attempt, releaseID?, epoch, run, wallTime, monotonicTime}`;
  - `submit-outcome {seq, startSeq, claimID, result, providerReference?}`;
  - `heartbeat {wallTime, monotonicTime}`, every minute;
  - `terminal {run, lastSeq, chainHash, kind: clean-shutdown | fenced-local | fenced-on-host}`, signed with the Core key. Only a process holding that volume's lock writes it.
- **The order of one attempt** across SQLite, the journal and the provider:
  1. SQLite transaction: claim the delivery (`preparing` → `submitting`, attempt n, lease, and a random 128-bit **`claimID`**). Commit.
  2. Journal: `submit-start` carrying the same `claimID`, then `fsync`.
  3. Provider I/O.
  4. Journal: `submit-outcome`, then `fsync`.
  5. SQLite transaction: the outcome. Commit.
- **Restart on the same volume:**
  - `submitting` with a `submit-start` of its `claimID` and no outcome → `outcomeUnknown`, never resent;
  - `submitting` with an outcome in the journal → that outcome is recorded;
  - `submitting` with no `submit-start` of its `claimID` → the provider was never called, because step 3 follows a durable step 2, so the attempt returns to `pending` without counting;
  - the new run's `run-start` names the previous run's last sequence and whether it closed with a `terminal` record.
- **Which journal entry is the last one.** A `clean-shutdown` record closes one run; the volume can start again, so it says nothing about later runs. Only a **fence record that binds the volume's final run, journal sequence and chain hash** shows that no later entry can exist: it is written under the volume's lock after the schema marker that stops every restart, by `restore` (`fenced-local`) or by `fence --local` on the old host (`fenced-on-host`) (§8.5.1). `owner-attested-stopped` binds no sequence, so it never supports a completeness proof.
- **Off-host copies** (`lisa core backup export`, `trust export`) copy the journal as it stands. Every backup records `s_cut`, the journal sequence at its cut, and the `claimID` of every delivery in `submitting` at the cut (§8.4.4).
- **Completeness proof for a previous writer W** after a cut:
  1. W's writer fence is `fenced-local` or `fenced-on-host`, and its record binds W's final run, sequence and chain hash;
  2. Lisa holds W's journal from the `run-start` of the run that was live at the cut (the run containing `s_cut`) — or from the volume's first `run-start`, for a volume created after the cut, such as an earlier restore's — through that final sequence, with an unbroken chain ending in the bound hash;
  3. every run boundary in that range is closed, by a `terminal` record or by a following `run-start` naming the last sequence.

  The replacement's own fence records never close W's journal.
- **Attempts open at the cut.** A delivery in `submitting` at the cut was claimed but not finished. Its `submit-start` may lie before `s_cut` or after it, and its provider may have accepted after the cut. The proof matches each such claim by its `claimID` within the proven range:
  - no `submit-start` with that `claimID` anywhere in the range → the provider was never called by that claim;
  - a `submit-start` and its outcome → that outcome (`acceptedByProvider` is final and never resent);
  - a `submit-start` and no outcome anywhere in the range → **`outcomeUnknown`**, held for the owner. An otherwise complete suffix never clears it, and neither does a later run's silence.

**Holds** are keyed by the slot `(occurrence, firing, recipient)` from `LisaFiringIdentity`, which is stable. A hold therefore survives regeneration of the slot from replayed work, ordinary `delivery resume`, and restarts; after a restart, holds are reloaded from the database before any channel resumes.
- **Sender uncertainty** holds:
  1. every backup row in `pending` or `inProgress` at the cut;
  2. every slot with a `submit-start` after `s_cut` in any journal Lisa holds, recreated as held if the restored database lacks it;
  3. every slot due in `U = (t_cut − margin, t_fence(channel) + margin]`, whether it is in the backup, missing from it, or first regenerated after resume. `t_fence(channel)` is when that channel's sender fence was established: credential rotation, or the stop time a writer fence records (§8.5.1). The margin (1 hour by default) widens to cover any wall-clock step that the old run's heartbeats show (wall time moving differently from monotonic time).

  A slot leaves this hold automatically only when, for **every** previous writer that could have sent it, a completeness proof exists and shows that, for that slot, there was no `submit-start`, or only starts whose recorded outcomes are definite refusals (R1's permanent failures), and that every claim on it open at the cut matched with no start lacking an outcome.
- **Local-ownership uncertainty** (push slots only). An assignment made after the cut is unknown to the restored database, and a device may already hold the slot as a local notification. Every device-notification slot due after the cut stays held until that device reports its assignment inventory after the restore (§8.10.4):
  - reported `scheduled`, `fired` or `outcome unknown` → device-owned, never pushed;
  - reported absent with its trigger still in the future → may be pushed once the sender-uncertainty hold is also clear;
  - no report → held until expiry or the owner's release.
- **Sender fences,** per channel: `writer-stopped` (implied by a closed writer fence, at its recorded time), `credentials-rotated` (the owner-recorded rotation time; for an OAuth mail channel, the grant revoked at the provider and authorized again, §8.19.1), or `unknown`. A channel whose sender fence is unknown doesn't resume (§8.5.4).
- **`lisa core delivery resume`** resumes the channels whose sender fences are known, and keeps every hold.
- **`lisa core delivery release <slot>`** shows the evidence and asks the owner to confirm: "The previous Lisa Core may already have sent this, and the provider may have accepted it. Sending again may duplicate it." For a push held for local ownership, it adds: "This device may also show it locally."
  - It writes a release record (`releaseID`) authorizing exactly one more attempt, and an audit record: actor, time, acknowledgement and the evidence shown.
  - That attempt follows the order above and R1's rules for one attempt (recipient, quiet hours, urgency). The claim in step 1 consumes the authorization, and the `submit-start` carries the `releaseID`.
  - A transient failure returns the slot to held, needing another release. An item past `validUntil` can't be released and shows as expired. No automatic retry ever follows.
  - The apps' Lisa Core status screen lists held items read-only.
- **A crash during a release.** The release record and the claim are durable. After the restart the journal shows a `submit-start` without an outcome, so the delivery becomes `outcomeUnknown` and the consumed authorization isn't reused. The owner sees "Released by you; outcome unknown — the provider may have accepted it. Lisa won't try again unless you release it again." A further release states that the earlier released attempt's outcome is unknown.
- **What the owner can establish about a released attempt whose own result is unknown.** Lisa never infers it. It records and shows:
  - the release, the claim and its journal start: time, channel, recipient and attempt number;
  - every provider reference it received before the interruption: the SMTP queue reply or the provider's message ID, the APNs `apns-id`, the Twilio call SID;
  - a **visible reference** that Lisa puts into every released message: "Lisa ref R-7Q4K" in an email's footer and in its `Message-ID`; in a push's `userInfo`, which the app reports back when it sees the alert; spoken at the end of a call. The owner can look for it in the inbox, in the device's notification history, or in the phone's call log;
  - for calls, `lisa core delivery lookup <slot>`: an owner-invoked, read-only query of Twilio's call log for that number and time window, through the configured account. Graph, Gmail, SMTP and APNs offer no such lookup.

  The owner records the finding with `lisa core delivery confirm <slot> --received`, which makes the slot `confirmedDelivered` by the owner (audited), or `--not-received`, which leaves it `outcomeUnknown`; another release then states that the earlier released attempt's result is still unknown.

**Recovery facts (delivery).** *Survives:* journals on reachable old volumes; fence records with the final positions they bind; off-host prefixes; `s_cut` and open `claimID`s in backups; device inventories; release records and visible references. *Who acts:* Lisa releases nothing automatically except on a completeness proof or a device's report; the owner releases slot by slot and records what the recipient side shows. *Safe after an interruption:* the attempt order above; a restart reloads holds and never resends. *What the owner sees while the outcome is unknown:* "Held — the previous Lisa Core on H1 may have sent this; no fence binds its last journal entry", "Held — an attempt started before the backup, and its result was never recorded", or "Held — waiting for <device> to report whether it shows this reminder".

**Acceptance (S-11, S-12; narrative §19.1)**
- **M3, portable, with scripted SMTP, Twilio and APNs servers and a scripted device client:**
  - **(a) An acceptance after the off-host copy.** Take backup A; copy the journal off-host; SMTP then accepts an email for slot O; lose the old volume; restore A, which appends its own fence record; O is regenerated from replayed work; run `delivery resume`. O will stay held at every step, and the owner will see the "no fence binds its last journal entry" evidence.
  - **(b) A genuine fence binding.** The same, except that the old volume survives and the owner runs `lisa core fence --local` on it, binding its final run and sequence. The complete journal shows no `submit-start` for slot Q due in U, so Q will be released automatically, while slots with a `submit-start` stay held.
  - **(c) A delegated firing.** After backup A, Core delegates firing 1 of reminder P to the phone, and the phone, offline, schedules it locally. Restore A with a complete, fenced journal showing zero APNs submissions. P will stay held for local ownership until the phone reports `scheduled`, then become device-owned and never be pushed. In a second run where the phone never reports, P will stay held until it expires.
  - **(d) A later run after a clean shutdown.** W shuts down cleanly, closing run r with `clean-shutdown`; the off-host copy includes that record; W starts again as run r+1, and SMTP accepts slot O2; W's volume is then lost. O2 will stay held: no fence binds W's final sequence, and the copy's `clean-shutdown` is shown as closing run r only. In the variant where W's volume survives, `fence --local` binds run r+1: O2's start and acceptance are found and O2 is never resent, while slot Q2, with no start in r or r+1, is released.
  - **(e) An attempt open at the cut.** W claims slot O3 and writes its `submit-start` before `s_cut`; backup A is taken; SMTP accepts; W crashes before `submit-outcome`, restarts, runs on, and is fenced on its host. The suffix from `s_cut` is complete and holds no outcome for O3. O3 will be `outcomeUnknown` and held, never released automatically. Variants: the claim committed before the cut with its `submit-start` after `s_cut` (the same result); the claim committed with no `submit-start` of its `claimID` anywhere in the fenced range (the provider was never called by that claim: released).
  - **(f) A reload after restart:** the restored Core is killed and restarted between `delivery review` and `resume`; every hold will be reloaded before any channel resumes.
  - **(g) Ordinary resume:** `delivery resume` will keep every hold of (a)–(e).
  - **(h) A crash during a release:** a release, then a kill after `submit-start`. After the restart: `outcomeUnknown`, no second attempt, the owner's message and the visible reference shown. `delivery confirm --received` will record the owner's finding; `--not-received` will leave the slot unknown, and a further release will warn as specified. For a released call, `delivery lookup` against the scripted Twilio server will find the call.
  - Also: a call and a push accepted after the cut, held as in (a); one released item sent once and audited; a backward wall-clock step in the old run's heartbeats widening U; a kill between each pair of steps of one attempt, recovered as the restart rules specify.
- **M4:** the same in the container.
- **M8, with the real device side** (`LisaNotificationOwnership` and the simulated notification center): (c) again, then the firing checks:
  - first display → explicit snooze → a delayed `fired(1)` report → exactly one later alert for firing 2;
  - a moved deadline keeping one occurrence and one pending request with a new generation;
  - a content-only edit with no new alert; close and reopen;
  - a reservation attempted while a push for the same firing is in flight;
  - a cleared alert; a disconnected device; a device ahead of a restored Core;
  - **a crash after the OS add and before success is recorded, with the alert fired and cleared before the restart:** `outcome unknown` while the firing is still valid, never added again. The same for a replacement, and for a cancellation whose trigger had passed;
  - **an old cancel recovered while a newer snooze is pending:** firing 1's `remove` intent is open at a crash, after the owner snoozed firing 1, so firing 2 is pending under the same identifier. After the restart the intent is superseded, firing 2's request stays, and firing 1 is `fired`;
  - **a restart after display, clearance and a backward clock step:** firing f fired and the owner cleared it; the clock is set back 30 minutes, before f's trigger; Lisa restarts with an open intent. f will be `outcome unknown` and never added again;
  - a delivered alert of firing 1 found while firing 2 is pending: `fired(1)` recorded, firing 2 untouched;
  - a command issued after a supplementary confirmation outranking one issued before it; a command under a confirmation the device hasn't verified waiting;
  - a snooze of an `outcome unknown` firing: f becomes `fired`, and f+1 is scheduled;
  - 40 eligible reminders, with the coverage hole shown as specified.
- **X6:** the same on devices, plus permission changes, Focus, previews and lock state (R2-A09–A12), with APNs run by the owner under the owner's account (§10.8).

### 8.11 Core deployment and operations (R2-CORE-01–05; finding 15; exposure and operations)

#### 8.11.1 Image and reproducible build

- **Build stage:** `swift:6.4.2-trixie@sha256:9733821c…430097`, running `swift build -c release --force-resolved-versions`, so the locked graph in `Package.resolved` is used unchanged.
- **Runtime stage:** `swift:6.4.2-trixie-slim@sha256:42571ee8…cccea0d`, plus Debian 13's SQLite, libxml2, libcurl, CA-certificate and time-zone packages.
  - These are installed from a fixed Debian snapshot: APT sources point at `snapshot.debian.org/archive/debian/<timestamp>` and `…/debian-security/<timestamp>` with `check-valid-until=no`, and every package is pinned as `name=version`.
  - The timestamp and versions are chosen and recorded at M4. This is in-image installation of selected prerequisites (P-03).
- **Provenance, split by what can know it:**
  - **Image labels record the inputs:** base image digests, the snapshot timestamp and package versions, the SHA-256 of `Package.resolved`, and the source commit. An image can't carry its own digest, because the labels are part of what the digest covers.
  - **An external build record holds the result:** the built image digest, with the build command, engine, context, architecture and date. It goes in the run directory and in `docs/VERIFICATION-SCENARIOS.md` (S-20), and the deployment instructions refer to it.

  Bit-for-bit reproducibility is not claimed; rebuilding from the same inputs is.
- **Platforms:** `linux/amd64` and `linux/arm64` are both declared. amd64 is built and run on the rootless engine. arm64 is `NOT_RUN_ENVIRONMENT` (Claude; missing platform), and a genuine cross-build would be a separate evidence entry (O-11).

#### 8.11.2 Container boundaries, secrets and SELinux

- **Boundaries:**
  - non-root user (UID 10001);
  - writable `/var/lib/lisa` (database, recovery ledger, run files) and a backups volume;
  - read-only `/etc/lisa`; `/run/secrets` from Compose file secrets; a bounded tmpfs for `/tmp`;
  - read-only root filesystem;
  - `cap_drop: ALL`, `no-new-privileges`, no privileged mode, no Docker socket;
  - no secret in a layer or build argument;
  - bounded logs (driver limits and Lisa's rotation);
  - `SIGTERM` handled within a stop grace period longer than Lisa's shutdown.
- **Secrets:** R1's checks stay as they are: the file is owned by the effective user, has no group or other bits, and is at most 64 KiB (§1.6).
  - Under this rootless engine, container UID 10001 is host UID 599824 (589824 + 10001 − 1). Secret files must therefore be owned by 599824 with mode 0400. `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md` sets this up with a one-off container run as container root (`chown 10001:10001`, `chmod 0400` inside it).
  - `lisa core doctor --secrets` reports owner and mode as the running process sees them.
- **Secret inventory for offline backup** (`docs/SECURITY.md`):
  - Core signing keys, current and retired;
  - export keys, current and retired;
  - TLS key, certificate, CA and intermediates bundle;
  - APNs key; SMTP and Twilio credentials; the model provider keys (OpenAI, Anthropic, an Ollama proxy token);
  - the iCloud app-specific password and the Proton Mail Bridge password and certificate (if P-09);
  - `provider-token-key`, which encrypts the provider-token store on the data volume (§8.19.1). Backups carry only that store's ciphertext.

  They are restored separately from database backups. Backup manifests list their references, never their values (R2-CORE-06).
- **SELinux:**
  - On this host, the rootless engine has no SELinux labelling (§1.2). It can show that Compose accepts the `:Z` mount options, not that containers are confined; that check is `NOT_RUN_ENVIRONMENT` here (Claude; environment configuration).
  - On the target host, the owner records the engine's security options, the container process label (`ps -eZ`, expecting `container_t`), and a denial when the container tries to read an unlabelled host path.
  - If the target engine has SELinux disabled, the record says confinement isn't demonstrated there.
  - SELinux is never disabled or relaxed with `label=disable`.

#### 8.11.3 VPN-only publishing (finding 15 preserved and strengthened)

**Required:** loopback-only development defaults; an explicit production bind or publish address; no silent fallback to all interfaces; IPv4 and IPv6 checks; a way to verify that a non-VPN client can't reach the API (R2-CORE-03).

**Mechanism**
- **Configuration (schema v2):** `api.bindAddresses`, a list of IP literals; R1's single `api.bindAddress` is read as a one-item list. Also `api.publishAddresses`, the VPN addresses the owner declares.
  - Outside a container, Lisa binds each configured address and refuses wildcards in production. An unset or empty list is a configuration error. Loopback is the development default.
- **One validated value inside and outside the container.** `deploy/compose.yaml` takes both the published host address and the container's `LISA_PUBLISH_ADDRESS` from `${LISA_VPN_ADDRESS:?set LISA_VPN_ADDRESS to the VPN address}`, so they can't differ, and an unset or empty value fails at render time.
  - IPv6 uses `LISA_VPN_ADDRESS6` in `deploy/compose.ipv6.yaml` the same way.
  - Inside the container Lisa binds the unspecified address of its own namespace only when:
    - `api.deployment = "container"`;
    - `LISA_PUBLISH_ADDRESS` is a non-empty, non-wildcard, non-loopback IP literal;
    - that address is listed in `api.publishAddresses`.

    Otherwise it refuses to start a listener.
- **Normal deployment runs the check before any port is published.** `deploy/lisa-deploy up` performs, in order:
  1. `docker compose config --format json`;
  2. `docker compose run --rm --no-deps -T lisa lisa core deploy check --rendered -`, which reads that rendered file. A plain `run` publishes no service ports. The check verifies that every published port has a host IP that is non-empty, non-wildcard, equal to `LISA_VPN_ADDRESS` or `LISA_VPN_ADDRESS6`, and listed in `api.publishAddresses`;
  3. only then, `docker compose up -d`.

  The documentation names this the only supported deployment command. Someone running `docker compose up` directly still meets the in-container refusal.
- **Evidence, kept in separate classes:**
  - **Rootless amd64, this host (M4):**
    - **Refusals:** unset, empty, wildcard, mismatched and loopback values (`127.0.0.2` among them) are refused by the render, the deploy check and the in-container check.
    - **The positive case** uses an address that passes every rule: non-loopback, non-wildcard, listed in `api.publishAddresses`, and **actually assigned to this host**. It is chosen at M4 from the host's own addresses, read without changing anything (`ip -br addr`): a private address (RFC 1918 or a ULA), never a public one. The published port will be observed listening only there (`ss -ltn`), and refused on the host's other addresses and on loopback. The listener runs only for the check and holds synthetic data. If this host has no such address, the positive case is `NOT_RUN_ENVIRONMENT` (Claude; environment configuration), and the refusals still run.
  - **Target host, by the owner (production-host class):** HTTPS reachable from a VPN client on the VPN address over IPv4, and over IPv6 if configured. Refused or unreachable from off-VPN clients on the host's LAN and public addresses over IPv4 and IPv6. Docker's forwarding chains inspected. Date, host and commands recorded. SELinux confinement is recorded separately (§8.11.2). Neither is inferred from the rootless runs.

#### 8.11.4 Health and freshness

- **Reported separately (R2-CORE-05):** liveness, database readiness, sync readiness, each source, the model, each delivery channel, storage, backup age, recovery stage, and sync lag per device. A failing news source never makes Core unready.
- **Publishing:** the running Core rewrites `<data>/run/health.json` every 15 seconds: `{runGeneration, sequence, wallTime, bootTimeNanos, components}`. `bootTimeNanos` is `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`, which is shared by processes on the host and continues through a stopped process.
- **One-shot probe:** `lisa core health --probe live|ready` reads only the lock file and `health.json`, and never opens the database (R1's `status` opened and wrote it, §1.6).
  - **Live:** the file's run generation equals the current lock holder's, and its age by `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` is at most 60 seconds.
  - **Ready:** live, plus database and sync readiness.
  - **After a restart:** the new run has a new generation, so the old file never counts as ready.
  - **Frozen or paused process:** the age grows, and the probe fails after 60 seconds.
  - **Wall-clock step back or forward:** no effect, because boot time is used.

  The container health check runs it every 30 seconds with 3 retries.
- **Restart sequence:**
  1. Recover transactions, the ready queue and waiting operations.
  2. Inspect uncertain outcomes, settling each `submitting` claim from the submission journal by its `claimID`: `outcomeUnknown` after a `submit-start` without an outcome, the recorded outcome if there is one, and back to `pending` if the provider was never called (§8.10.6). Holds are reloaded before any channel resumes.
  3. Reconcile schedules and assignments.
  4. Suppress obsolete work.
  5. Resume bounded processing.
  6. Report degraded capabilities truthfully.

  A persistent provider failure is a health state, not a restart loop.

#### 8.11.5 Administrative modes

Every `lisa core` command has exactly one mode (§8.12.3 lists them). A mode is defined by what the command may touch:

| Mode | Live state (the database under the data directory) | Recovery ledger, lock and `health.json` | Backup sets, archives and output files | Owner listener | Lock |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **listener** | Only through the running Core | Only through the running Core | Writes only output files the owner names (for example `trust export --to`) | Required: the loopback listener named in `run/owner-endpoint.json` (owner-only file, PID checked against the lock holder, as R1 does); works in recovery mode where the stage allows | Not taken |
| **offline** | Reads and writes directly | Reads and appends | Reads and writes backup sets and the owner's named outputs | Not used; refuses while Core runs | Exclusive data-directory lock |
| **files** | **Never opened** | Read only | Reads them; writes only into locations the owner names, or the record a command defines inside a backup set (`validation.json`) | Not used for R2. `lisa archive reconcile` uses R1's owner listener (§8.14.8) | Not taken |

No command opens the database just to report status.

**Acceptance (M3 portable, M4 container; S-15, S-20):** the checks will cover:
- the publishing cases above, with the positive case on an assigned private address and `127.0.0.2` among the refusals;
- the image labels holding the inputs, and the external build record holding the digest that `docker image inspect` reports;
- each command touching only what its mode allows (a files-mode command run against a live data directory with the database file made unreadable, which must still work);
- the UID mapping and secret ownership and modes seen inside the container;
- the read-only root refusing writes, with the data and backup volumes writable;
- readiness after a restart reflecting only the new generation;
- a frozen container's probe failing after 60 seconds;
- a backward wall-clock step of one hour causing no false freshness or staleness;
- each command refusing the wrong mode;
- R2-D01–D04, D06–D09 and D12 on rootless amd64;
- D05 and SELinux confinement on the target host.

### 8.12 API and CLI (R2-API-01–04; finding 1; real v1 compatibility)

#### 8.12.1 The v2 API

- **Negotiation:** every v2 request carries the protocol version and client features. `GET /v2/capabilities` returns:
  - API and schema versions and features;
  - the dataset, the head (epoch and confirmation), trust and policy versions;
  - the backup catalogue R, the recovery stage and the ready-queue length;
  - redacted health.

  An unknown required capability gets `upgrade_required`.
- **Routes:**

| Operation | Route |
|---|---|
| Capabilities and health | `GET /v2/capabilities` |
| Enrollment and trust bootstrap | `POST /v2/enrollments`; `GET /v2/trust/tls-anchors` (§8.1.2) |
| Trust and policy | `GET /v2/trust` (paged artifacts, §8.1.3); `GET /v2/trust/nearby-secret` (Design A) |
| Challenges and recovery evidence | `POST /v2/challenges`; `POST /v2/recovery/evidence` (§8.1.5, §8.5.3) |
| Trust bundles from devices whose keys Core doesn't know | `POST /v2/recovery/trust-bundles`, authorized by a one-time evidence code (§8.5.3) |
| Credentials and keys | `POST /v2/devices/current/credential-reset`; `…/credential-refresh`; `…/key-update` |
| Submit operations | `POST /v2/operations` (at most 100 operations and 512 KiB of signed bytes, per-operation outcomes) |
| Receipt lookup | `POST /v2/receipts/lookup` |
| Incremental changes | `GET /v2/changes?partitions=&after=` (signed pages, §8.4.2) |
| Snapshots | `POST /v2/snapshots`, `GET /v2/snapshots/{id}`, `GET /v2/snapshots/{id}/chunks/{n}`; bucket snapshots by partition |
| Blobs | `PUT` and `GET /v2/blobs/{digest}/chunks/{n}`, `GET /v2/blobs/{digest}` |
| Replication checkpoint and custody | `PUT /v2/devices/current/replication` |
| Conflicts | `GET /v2/conflicts`; resolution is an operation or group (§8.3.2) |
| Working set | `GET /v2/working-set`, `GET /v2/working-set/{collection}` |
| Notifications | `GET /v2/devices/current/notification-assignments`; `POST /v2/devices/current/notification-reports` |
| Push registration, permission, device preferences, receipts | as in v1, under `/v2/devices/current/…` |
| Queue imports and their read-only lookups (through `R1RequestEvidenceReading`) | `POST /v2/migration/imports` (manifest), `POST /v2/migration/imports/{id}/items`, `GET /v2/migration/imports/{id}` (classes and outcomes) (§8.14.4); legacy link in enrollment (§8.14.5). Lookups run inside Core under a lookup grant or the link; no route converts without the conversion authority |
| Rollback | `GET /v2/rollback`; `POST /v2/rollback/acknowledgements` (§8.14.7) |
| Delivery holds (owner, read-only) | `GET /v2/delivery/holds` |
| Revocation | `DELETE /v2/devices/{id}` (owner) |
| Export | `GET /v2/export` (owner; includes waiting, rejected and quarantined work) |

- **Errors (R2-API-03):** typed codes for malformed, unauthenticated, forbidden, revoked device, expired trust, wrong dataset, `epoch_mismatch`, `trust_recovery`, `rollback_in_progress`, `upgrade_required`, `dependency_missing`, `dependency_cycle`, `conflict`, oversized, throttled, `insufficient_storage`, `cursor_expired`, `credential_invalidated_by_restore`, `unverifiable_destination` (client-side), `enrollment_*` (including `enrollment_response_purged`), `trust_bundle_invalid`, `trust_bundle_incomplete`, `evidence_code_expired` and temporary failure. On v1, R2-specific outcomes travel as `lisaCode` beside one of R1's ten codes (`upgrade_required`, `reply_unavailable`, `awaiting_administrative_review`, §8.12.2).
  - Batches answer per operation, and a client checkpoint never moves past changes it hasn't installed.
  - `GET /v2/tasks` stays a typed 404.
- **Size limits** are enforced on streamed bytes (§8.16.2).

#### 8.12.2 v1 translation and the one legacy converter (findings 1 and 3; real v1 compatibility)

**Required:** R1 clients must not mutate through a path that bypasses the R2 ledger; a clear `upgrade_required` is acceptable; no silent unsafe reinterpretation (R2-API-01). One operation ID has one digest everywhere (R2-SYNC-02).

**Decision:** safe route-by-route v1 compatibility (O-4).

**Wire rules for frozen R1 clients (§1.6)**
- Every v1 response carries `Lisa-API-Version: 1.0`. A major version other than 1 would make R1 treat every answer, including successes, as "may have been applied".
- Error bodies use only R1's ten codes with a message. R2 adds a `lisaCode` field, which R1 ignores. An unknown code would make R1 discard the whole body.
- Routes, DTO shapes and status codes are R1's. `GET /v2/tasks` from a v1 client stays a typed 404.

**One authoritative conversion.** `LisaLegacyRequests` is the only code that turns an R1 request into R2 effects. It runs at Core only, for every path a legacy request can take:

| Path | Authority, bound into the parent | Scope |
|---|---|---|
| A frozen R1 client over v1 | The legacy device credential, or R1's owner credential on the loopback owner listener | `device:<id>` or `owner` |
| A queue import from the upgraded Mac (§8.14.4) | The owner's **conversion approval** for that import, bound to its manifest digest and the entries it lists | `owner` |
| A queue import from an upgraded iPhone or iPad (§8.14.5) | The **legacy link**: the device's R1 credential, verified at enrollment, together with the queue-manifest digest it declared | `device:<legacy ID>` |
| An R1 ledger record at migration | The record itself, as an **outcome-only parent** (below). Nothing is converted: R1 already applied it, and its effects are in the migration origin | as recorded |

A **lookup grant** (§8.14.4) authorizes read-only evidence lookups and nothing else; it never authorizes a conversion.

**The parent** — Core-only, never replicated, keyed by `(scope, requestID)` — holds:
- the authority above and the origin path (v1, import or migrated);
- R1's fingerprint: **recomputed by Core from the original bytes** when Core has them (a declared fingerprint is never trusted), or the one R1 recorded, for a migrated parent. It is kept for the life of the dataset and **never replicated**. The original bytes are kept until the parent is complete and are subject to deletion like other content;
- **the child set:** for a parent created from bytes, complete and fixed at creation — every discriminator the request implies, in request order, with its child ID and, once decided, its disposition: `applied`, `applied_with_conflict`, `already_present` (R1's permanent capture key found an earlier capture, including one R1 created, which is returned as R1 returns it), `refused` (R1's status and reason), `not_reached` (after an earlier refusal, where R1 stops), `held_for_administrative_review` or `pending_behind_review` (below). For an outcome-only migrated parent the child set is `unknown` until bytes attach;
- **R1's actual outcome:** for a migrated record, R1's status, body and attribution (§8.14.3); otherwise the outcome produced under R1's semantics;
- the recorded status and body, until the body expires (`api.idempotencyRetentionDays`, default 30) or is invalidated, with the reply dependency index (below);
- progress, for the one route committed in steps (receipts, below), and the state `open`, `awaiting_administrative_review` or `complete`.

The compact form (everything except bodies and original bytes) lives as long as the dataset.

**Children are a pure function of the request.**
- **IDs:** the first 16 bytes of `SHA-256("lisa-v1-child-v1" ‖ 0x00 ‖ len32(dataset) ‖ dataset ‖ len32(scope) ‖ scope ‖ len32(requestID) ‖ requestID ‖ len32(discriminator) ‖ discriminator)`, where `len32` is a big-endian 32-bit length.
- **Bodies** depend only on the dataset and the original request: the target entity, the field and value, and R1's `expectedRevision` taken from the request ("observed at projection revision N"). An entity the request creates gets an ID derived the same way. Nothing in a body comes from current state. The same request therefore yields the same children, byte for byte, whichever path brings it and whenever, in whichever epoch, it is converted.
- **Every envelope field is fixed by the request too.** The origin is Core's legacy-translation origin, with incarnation `SHA-256("lisa-v1-incarnation-v1" ‖ len32(dataset) ‖ dataset ‖ len32(scope) ‖ scope ‖ len32(requestID) ‖ requestID)` (first 16 bytes) and sequence `index + 1`. The protocol, schema and policy versions are those of the derivation scheme (`lisa-v1-child-v1`), and the creation time is left out; displays use the parent's first receipt time. Only the signature, which isn't part of the digest (§8.2.1), can differ between conversions, for example after a key rotation.
- **Core signs every child** under that origin, with `legacyParent {scope, requestID, discriminator, index, count, setDigest}`. `count` is the size of the complete child set, and `setDigest` is the SHA-256 of its ordered child IDs.
- **Dependencies are bounded.** A child depends only on its **immediate predecessor** among the request's children that touch the same entity (for preferences, the same key): at most one dependency, however often the request repeats a key. Same-entity order is therefore a chain, children of different entities don't depend on each other, and the 32-dependency limit (§8.2.2) is never approached. R1's stop at the first refusal is expressed in the dispositions Core decides, not in replica dependencies.
- **Decisions are made per epoch.** In one transaction Core creates every child and decides each, in request order, under R1's semantics against current state: the revision check, validation, R1's stop at the first refusal (`not_reached` after it), and the administrative hold below. Each decided child gets a Core-signed final receipt carrying its own digest and commit position. Two epochs can decide the same child differently; its bytes never differ.
- **The parent is complete** when every child has a final disposition and the response is recorded.

| Route | Discriminators |
|---|---|
| Task or idea PATCH | `field:<name>` for each changed field (an idea's tags among them), then `status` or `lifecycle` |
| Preferences PATCH | `change:<index>` in request order (R1 allows one key many times, up to 50 changes) |
| Notification action | `receipt` (paired-device callers only), then `action`, then `task-complete` when the action is "complete" |
| Reminder action | `action`, then `task-complete` |
| Notification receipts | `receipt:<index>:<notificationID>`, one per ID in request order |
| Task create; capture | `create:task`; `create:capture`, then `create:idea` or `create:news` |
| Any other single-operation route | `single` |

**Administrative children wait, and so does everything after them.** R2-TRUST-05 keeps administrative keys CLI-only, and R1 applies a preferences request in order and stops at the first refusal, so whether the rest runs depends on the decision about the held change.
- **Owner scope** (the loopback v1 owner listener, or a Mac import): when conversion reaches an administrative key, that child is `held_for_administrative_review`, and every later child is `pending_behind_review` — neither applied nor refused. Earlier children are decided as usual. The parent is `awaiting_administrative_review`.
- **The decision is CLI-only:** `lisa core legacy review` lists waiting parents; `lisa core legacy resolve <scope> <requestID> apply|refuse` decides one at the administrative terminal.
  - **apply:** the held child is applied with the authority of that decision, which its receipt records. The pending suffix is then decided in order under R1's semantics against current state; a later administrative key holds again.
  - **refuse:** the held child is `refused` with R1's 403 shape (code `forbidden`, message "This setting can be changed only on the administrative terminal."), every later child becomes `not_reached`, and that 403 is recorded as the request's answer.
- **Device scope:** R1 itself refuses owner-only keys and deletes from devices (§1.6), so the child is `refused` (403) at once, later children are `not_reached`, and nothing waits.
- **A frozen v1 caller** — the request that reached the hold, and every retry while the parent waits — gets HTTP 503 with R1 code `unavailable`, `retryAfterSeconds: 300`, `lisaCode: "awaiting_administrative_review"` and the message "This change is waiting for review on the administrative terminal." It is never recorded, as R1 never records a 503. The frozen queue keeps the entry pending (retry later, not uncertain), and the frozen CLI reports R1's "unavailable" message. The first retry after the decision gets the recorded answer.
- **An import item** whose parent waits reports `awaiting_administrative_review` with the parent reference. Discarding an import entry before conversion creates nothing; a later v1 retry then converts it under its own authority and waits at the same child.

**Restricted siblings.** A child whose entity is outside the replication allowlist (§8.17) — for example a change to `model.endpoint` or `model.dataSharing` inside a preferences request — is a **restricted sibling**. Core never replicates its body. Its receipt replicates in redacted form: `{childID, index, disposition, commitPosition, opaqueCommitment}`, signed by Core, where `opaqueCommitment = SHA-256("lisa-legacy-restricted-v1" ‖ salt ‖ childDigest)` and the random 32-byte salt stays at Core. Core can open a commitment for an administrative audit by showing the salt and the child.

**What a replica or relay can check by itself:**
- the Core signature, through the key history;
- every sibling's ID, from `(dataset, scope, requestID, discriminator)`. For preferences the discriminators are `change:<index>`, so every ID follows from `count` without any key or value;
- that all visible siblings carry the same `scope`, `requestID`, `count` and `setDigest`, and that the set digest matches the IDs;
- the index order and the same-entity chains;
- each visible child's receipt digest against its body, and each restricted sibling's signed, redacted receipt.

It sees no restricted key or value, and no hash over one. R1's fingerprint, a SHA-256 over the whole request body, could be tested against guessed values, so it stays in the parent. The conversion against the original R1 bytes is checked only at Core and rests on Core's signature.

**Outcome-only migrated parents.** R1's ledger record holds only the scoped key, fingerprint, status, response body and expiry (§1.6): no request bytes and no per-change outcome. Migration therefore creates an **outcome-only parent**: scope, request ID, R1's fingerprint, R1's status, R1's body (kept or invalidated by the migrated-body rule below, and re-rendered if it is an error), expiry, origin `migrated`, and **an unknown child set**. No child operation exists for it: R1 already applied the request, and its effects are in the migration origin.
- **Replay:** the same key and fingerprint get R1's recorded answer, or the terminal reply below; another fingerprint gets 422 `requestIDReused`.
- **Bytes attach only on verified identity:** an import item, or a v1 retry, in the same scope whose bytes Core hashes to the parent's fingerprint. Core then derives the child set from the bytes and records each child's R1 history from R1's answer: `applied_in_r1` for a 2xx; `refused_in_r1` for a refusal attributed before any commit (§8.14.3); `unknown_in_r1` where R1's message can't say which change it stopped at. Still no operation is created. Bytes with another fingerprint are never attached.
- **Unknown history stays visible:** the review shows "R1 answered 409 for this request; which of its 3 changes R1 applied is unknown", with each requested value beside the current one.

**Atomicity and R1's durable prefixes**
- One R2 transaction commits the parent, every decided child, the projections and the recorded response. R1's follow-up commits (planning, reminder sync) become durable follow-up work after that commit, so R1's late 503 and 500 answers after a commit can't happen, and a `createTask` retry can't create a second task (§1.6). A decision on a held child is one further transaction of the same shape.
- **Receipts keep R1's durable prefix.** R1 records receipts one write at a time, and a storage failure leaves the earlier ones committed (§1.6). R2 does the same. The first transaction creates the parent with the complete child set and commits receipt 0; each later receipt is its own transaction that also advances the parent's progress. After a storage failure the earlier receipts stay, and the caller gets R1's unrecorded 503 or 500. A retry with the same key and fingerprint continues from the recorded progress, and the last transaction records the 204. Unknown IDs are ignored, as in R1.
- **Projection revisions** advance exactly as R1's would: one step per R1 service call that changed something (correction +1, status +1; edit +1, lifecycle +1; each preference change +1). v1 `expectedRevision` semantics are unchanged.

**Route by route**

| v1 route | Children committed (R1's boundaries reproduced) | Recorded and answered |
|---|---|---|
| `POST /v1/plans/{p}/blocks/{b}/actions` | One plan-block operation; validation and conflicts before it | R1 status; RundownDTO |
| `POST /v1/briefs/regenerate` | None: a Core command; a failed composition is recorded as R1 records it | BriefDTO, or R1's error |
| `POST /v1/captures` | The capture with its idea or news item, IDs derived from the parent. R1's permanent caller-scoped deduplication is kept through the capture request key in records and tombstones, which also covers captures R1 created | 201 or 200 CaptureDTO (no text) |
| `POST /v1/captures/{id}/retrieve` | Core command with libcurl's checks | R1's statuses |
| `POST /v1/tasks` | One create-task, with the task ID derived from the parent | 201 TaskDTO; recorded atomically |
| `PATCH /v1/tasks/{id}` | Revision check (409 "the task changed since it was read"), then all correction fields validated together (400, nothing committed), then field children, then the status child. A refused status (409 "the task cannot move from …") leaves the field children committed with the error recorded | R1's status and body |
| `PATCH /v1/ideas/{id}` | The same shape: edit children, then the lifecycle child (409 "the idea cannot move from …") | R1's |
| `PATCH /v1/preferences` | One child per change in order. The first refusal (403 owner-only key or device delete, 400 value, 409 revision, 404) stops the request: earlier children stay, later ones are `not_reached`. In owner scope an administrative key holds the request instead (above) | R1's, including the full preference list on success; the 503 above while held |
| `POST /v1/reminders/{id}/actions` | The occurrence-firing action; "complete" adds the task completion | R1's |
| `POST /v1/notifications/{id}/actions` | Receipt (paired devices), then the action, then a task completion if any. A refusal after the receipt (403, 404, 409) keeps the receipt | R1's |
| `POST /v1/event-proposals/{id}/actions` | Review or correction | R1's |
| `PUT …/push-registration`, `…/notification-status`, `PATCH …/notification-preferences` | Legacy device-local records | R1's; an R2-enrolled device gets `upgrade_required` |
| `POST …/notification-receipts` | One child per ID, committed one by one with the parent's progress, so R1's durable prefix is kept | 204; an R2-enrolled device gets `upgrade_required` |
| `DELETE /v1/devices/{id}` | Owner: revocation (roster updated). Legacy device: unpairs itself | R1's |
| `POST /v1/devices/pairing-codes`, `POST /v1/devices/pair` | Legacy devices only, outside the ledger as in R1, because their answers carry secrets | not recorded |
| GET routes | Kept, reading projections | R1's |

**Error bodies never echo caller input.** R1 interpolates caller input into some errors it records (§1.6): an unknown notification category name, an unknown weekday token, an unsupported preference key (in both `field` and message), and an owner-only key named in a 403. R2 keeps R1's status, code and body shape, and renders every message from a fixed template that names positions, never caller text:
- "changes[3].key is not a supported preference", with `field: "changes[3].key"`; "changes[1].value has an unknown weekday", with `field: "changes[1].value"`; "categories[2] is not a known notification category", with `field: "categories"`; "changes[2] can be changed only by the owner on the host";
- live v1 answers use these templates directly;
- **migrated** R1 error bodies are re-rendered at migration from R1's status, code and the template that matches R1's message; a message that matches none of R1's templates becomes the generic template for its code ("The request was refused."), and R1's text is not kept;
- **reversed** records hold only sanitized bodies (§8.14.8).

Status and code are R1's, so every frozen client classifies the answer exactly as before; only the human text differs.

**Retries, expiry and the reply dependency index**
- **Same key and fingerprint:** the recorded response, with `Lisa-Replayed: true`. **Different fingerprint:** 422 `requestIDReused`.
- **Recorded errors** (400, 403, 404, 409, 413) are kept for the parent's life and replayed exactly. Rendered from templates, they hold no caller text or owner content, so they never need invalidation.
- **The reply dependency index.** When a success body is recorded, the DTO mapping reports every entity and preference key whose content the body contains, not only the request's target; a preferences PATCH answer, for example, lists every preference. Deleting, expiring or redacting any of them invalidates the body **in the same transaction**, never at maintenance. After "change preference A", "delete preference B" and a replay of A's request, the replay therefore can't reveal B.
- **Migrated parents** have no index: R1 recorded none and never redacted (§1.6). At migration, an R1 success body is kept only if R1's audit shows no `data.deleted` after the body was recorded and the audit horizon covers that time; otherwise it is invalidated. After migration, the first deletion, expiry or redaction of any owner content invalidates every migrated body at once, through one dataset-level marker written in that transaction.
- **After a success body has expired or been invalidated,** the mutation is never repeated, and the answer is a **terminal reply** that every real R1 caller can decode (§1.6). The same table serves live v1 answers, migrated parents, and the records reverse migration writes into R1's own ledger (§8.14.8):

| Route | Terminal reply, in every scope | What each frozen caller does with it |
|---|---|---|
| `POST /v1/captures` | 200 with a `CaptureDTO` built from the capture or its tombstone, `created: false`, idea and news IDs omitted where those items were deleted. `CaptureDTO` carries no text by its shape | The typed client decodes it; `lisa capture --request-id` prints "already saved …" and exits 0; the queue marks the entry done |
| `POST /v1/captures/{id}/retrieve` | 409 `conflict`, R1's own answer to a second retrieval | The CLI reports R1's conflict; never queued |
| `POST /v1/tasks` | 409 `conflict`, `lisaCode: "reply_unavailable"`: "This task was created earlier; its reply is no longer available." | The typed client decodes the error, and nothing is created again. R1 never queues task creation |
| `PATCH /v1/tasks/{id}`, `PATCH /v1/ideas/{id}`; reminder, notification, proposal and plan-block actions | These bodies hold only their subject's content, so they are invalidated only when the subject is deleted: 404 `notFound`, R1's own "the task does not exist" (or "the idea", "the reminder", …) | The typed client decodes the error; the queue marks the entry failed with R1's message, which is true |
| `PATCH /v1/preferences`; `POST /v1/briefs/regenerate` | 409 `conflict`, `lisaCode: "reply_unavailable"`: "This change was applied; its reply is no longer available. Reload to see current values." | The typed client decodes the error; the queue marks the entry as a conflict, and the owner reloads |
| Device-scoped routes (notification preferences, the device record) | The caller's own current device preferences or device record, which aren't owner content | Decoded as R1 decodes them |
| Receipts and other 204 routes | 204, empty | Done |

  Within one scope the terminal reply is the same on every retry, and a deleted item's content never appears, even within the retention period. No terminal reply is `{}`.

**`upgrade_required` on v1**
- **When:** a changed field has an unresolved R2 conflict, which a v1 client can neither see nor resolve; the caller is an R2-enrolled device; or the request has a shape with no R2 operation.
- **Answer:** HTTP 426, body `{"code":"forbidden","message":"Update Lisa on this device to make this change.","lisaCode":"upgrade_required"}`, recorded on the parent.
- **What the frozen R1 app shows (§1.6):**
  - "That didn't work: Update Lisa on this device to make this change.";
  - an action submitted while connected is marked `failed` and discarded at once, so only that message appears;
  - an action queued earlier appears under "Didn't Go Through" with Try Again and Discard on iPhone and iPad, or only in Diagnostics on the Mac;
  - Try Again re-queues under a new request ID and gets 426 again, with no effect.
- **A stale `expectedRevision`** (an old conflict in R1's sense) stays R1's 409 `conflict` with `currentRevision`, shown as "This item changed on your host."

**Acceptance (M3, the frozen R1 client, queue and CLI built from `491c90a`; S-01, with the conversion cases in S-03):**
- a multi-field PATCH with one bad field: nothing committed, 400 recorded;
- a valid correction followed by a refused status: field children committed with the 409 recorded, matching R1's database state field by field;
- a preferences PATCH repeating one key, and one refused at the third change (the later children `not_reached`);
- **receipts:** a storage failure injected at the third of five receipts. The first two will stay committed, as in R1, and the caller will get an unrecorded 503; the retry will continue from the third and end with 204; unknown IDs will be ignored;
- **one request converted in two epochs:** a v1 request applied in E1 after backup A; A restored; the same request retried. The children will have identical IDs and bodies, a replica holding E1's copies will record duplicates rather than identity reuse, and the change will apply once;
- **every terminal reply against the frozen callers:** each reply in the table will be decoded by the frozen typed client without a decoding error; `lisa capture --request-id X`, rerun after expiry and after the capture's deletion, prints "already saved …" and exits 0; `--retrieve` rerun gets R1's 409; a typed-client `createTask` replay gets `reply_unavailable`, and no second task exists; the frozen queue ends a task PATCH whose task was deleted as failed with "the task does not exist", and a preferences PATCH whose answer was invalidated as a conflict, with nothing under "Didn't Go Through" applied twice;
- **error privacy:** a preferences PATCH whose third change names the synthetic key `canary-key-7f3e` with the weekday token `canaryday`, and a notification-preferences PATCH naming the category `canary-category`. Each answer will keep R1's status, code and `field` position, decode in the frozen client, and contain none of the canary strings; a replay will return the same sanitized bytes; a migrated R1 error body holding a canary will be re-rendered at migration; after expiry nothing will remain; and the canaries will be absent from the database, its WAL, backups, exports and a reversed v4 database (S-19);
- change preference A, delete preference B, then replay A's request before daily maintenance: nothing of B in the reply (S-19);
- a migrated R1 body recorded before an R1 preference deletion: invalidated at migration; another migrated body invalidated by the first deletion after migration;
- a create retried after an edit, after a deletion (the response checked to contain nothing of the item), and after body expiry;
- a stale revision (409) and a conflicted field (426, with the frozen client's message and queue state checked);
- an R2-enrolled device on v1 (426);
- a crash between commit and response, then a retry;
- both pairing routes leaving no record.

For every case, a database diff will show the mutation applied at most once.

#### 8.12.3 CLI (R2-API-04)

| Group | Command | Mode (§8.11.5) |
|---|---|---|
| Lifecycle | `init` | offline |
| | `serve` | offline (holds the lock) |
| | `health --probe live\|ready` | files |
| | `status` | listener; if no Core runs, a files-only summary from `health.json` and the lock |
| | `doctor [--secrets] [--tls] [--config] [--connectors]` | files |
| Devices | `device enroll [--list] [--cancel]`, `device list`, `device revoke`, `device reset-credential` | listener |
| Trust | `trust review`, `trust confirm`, `trust import` | listener (recovery stages) |
| | `trust evidence-code`, `trust import-evidence --file <bundle>`, `trust set-aside <bundle>` (§8.5.3) | listener (recovery stages) |
| | `trust export`, `trust export-key` (the public `lisa-export-key:1:` text, §8.6.2) | listener |
| | `trust reanchor-text <device>` | listener |
| | `trust import-key --file <key backup>` | offline |
| Fencing (§8.5.1) | `fence --local [--export-tail <dir>]` (on a previous writer's own host and volume, after its Core stopped) | offline on that volume |
| | `fence import <file>`, `fence attest --stopped`, `fence note --client-isolated <volume>`, `fence status` | listener (recovery stages) |
| Sync | `sync status`, `operations pending`, `conflicts list`, `conflicts show` | listener |
| Backup | `backup` | listener (online backup through Core), or offline |
| | `backup validate [--anchor <dir> \| --anchor-fingerprint <fp>]`, `backup inspect <id>`, `backup scan --root …`, `backup export --to …` | files (`validate` writes `validation.json` into the backup set) |
| | `backup pin` (reports prune floors), `backup retain <n>`, `backup coverage <id>` (per-holder ranges, §8.4.5) | listener |
| | `restore <id> [--reanchor]` | offline |
| Migration | `migrate inspect --r1-data-dir …` | files (read-only on R1's directory) |
| | `migrate prepare --r1-data-dir …` | offline on R1's directory (takes R1's lock) |
| | `migrate run` | offline |
| | `migrate import list\|review <id>`, `migrate import approve-lookups <id> --digest <words>`, `migrate import approve-conversion <id> --entries <list> --digest <words>` | listener |
| | `migrate rollback --preflight`, `migrate rollback --fence` | listener |
| | `migrate rollback --reverse` | offline (after the fence) |
| | `migrate verify-r1 --copy` | files (runs the frozen R1 binary and frozen R1 clients on a disposable copy) |
| Legacy requests (§8.12.2) | `legacy review`, `legacy resolve <scope> <requestID> apply\|refuse` | listener |
| Connectors (§8.19.1) | `connector authorize <provider> <account>` (prints the consent URL; takes the redirected address, or receives it on a loopback port), `connector status`, `connector revoke <account>` | listener |
| Delivery | `delivery status`, `suspend`, `review`, `resume`, `release <slot>`, `confirm <slot> --received\|--not-received`, `lookup <slot>` (read-only call log, §8.10.6) | listener |
| Import | `import-journal <archive>` | listener |
| Diagnostics | `protocol decode`, `protocol verify` | files |
| Deployment | `deploy check --rendered -` | files |
| Post-downgrade | `lisa archive inspect`, `lisa archive reconcile`, `lisa archive revoke-device <id>`, `lisa archive log` (§8.14.8) | files; reconciliation applies through R1's owner listener, reads R1's database read-only for proofs, and keeps its own reconcile store |

Output redacts secrets and private content. R1's existing commands stay, for R1 data and tools.

### 8.13 Apple applications and Siri (R2-UI-01–05)

- **All three apps** offer Today, Brief, Inbox and review, Ideas, Memory, Settings and health, Conflicts and Diagnostics. When Core is unreachable, the app is never replaced by an error screen.
- **Mac (O-9):** a client only. It links LisaClientCore, LisaPresentation and the shared Apple sources, and no Core module.
  - It runs in the App Sandbox with entitlements for network client and server (nearby listening), user-selected read-write files (export destinations; the old-queue import is opened read-only, §8.14.4) and push.
  - It enrolls like the other devices, registers for APNs, schedules delegated local notifications and takes part in nearby sync.
  - Physical checks at X3 and X7: Keychain access in the data-protection keychain, listening under the sandbox, signing with a provisioning profile.
- **iPhone and iPad:** one universal app; a three-column split view with keyboard shortcuts on iPad, tabs and navigation stacks on iPhone.
- **Status language (R2-UI-02):** "Saved on this device", "Also held by <device>", "Synced with Core", "Waiting to sync", "Needs review", "Using saved calendar information", "Local brief", "Notifications scheduled through …", "Only on this device: n changes". Counters, hashes, versions and transport details appear only in Diagnostics.
- **Conflicts (R2-UI-03):** the item and field; each candidate with the device it came from; the explanation; whether planning or reminders are affected. The owner picks or edits, and a draft resolution survives new arrivals (§8.3.2). No raw JSON.
- **Permissions and accessibility (R2-UI-04):**
  - Notification, local-network and camera permission, Keychain failures, lost enrollment, provider errors and background limits each get a clear state and a recovery path.
  - Declared keys: `NSLocalNetworkUsageDescription`, `NSBonjourServices` (`_lisa-sync._tcp`), `NSCameraUsageDescription` (§8.1.2), `BGTaskSchedulerPermittedIdentifiers`, and only the `fetch` background mode.
  - Accessibility: VoiceOver labels, Dynamic Type, keyboard navigation on Mac and iPad, light and dark appearance, readable empty and error states.
- **App icon:** derived from `assets/logo.png` (1536 × 1024); the centered 1024 × 1024 crop is the candidate for the owner's visual review at X3 (O-9).
- **Privacy manifest:** added; R1 had none.
- **Siri (R2-UI-05):** the six R1 intents call LisaClientCore's durable commands. Replies distinguish completed on this device, waiting to sync, committed by Core (when known), needs clarification, unavailable (including "Unlock your iPhone to use Lisa") and failed. None reports success before the local save. The alarm automation reads a prepared canonical or local brief and never needs a live model; alarm-start, stopped and snoozed triggers are documented as observed on the owner's iPhone.

### 8.14 Migration, cutover and rollback (R2-MIG-01–04; findings 2, 3 and 13; migration authority and backups)

#### 8.14.1 Preparation

1. **The owner stops every R1 writer** and disables its automatic start: the Mac app's login item and any `lisa serve` service, following `docs/MIGRATION-R1-R2.md`.
2. **`lisa core migrate prepare --r1-data-dir <dir>`** takes R1's own data-directory lock and holds it to the end. Under the lock it:
   1. inventories R1's schema and application version, identifiers, secret references (names only), source cursors, schedules, queues, deliveries with their unknown outcomes, device registrations, the audit horizon, and any `data.deleted` events naming captures;
   2. **records the intended dataset ID** (R2-MIG-01 step 5) in the migration record;
   3. writes and validates the **rollback backup**: R1's database at schema v4, plus `runtime-state.json`, `model-requests.json` and `dry-run/captures.jsonl`, with SHA-256 digests in a manifest that also lists the configuration file's digest and secret references;
   4. **fences** R1's database with a schema version R1 refuses;
   5. writes the **migration input**: a backup of the fenced database with the same files.

   No write can happen between the backups and the fence.
3. **If the R1 data is on a Mac** (embedded host; default `~/.local/share/lisa`, from `~/.config/lisa/config.json5`):
   - the owner stops the app, copies the stopped data directory to the server, and runs `prepare` there against the copy;
   - the owner's confirmation that no R1 software can start on the Mac goes into the migration record;
   - the R2 `lisa` for macOS, if it exists after X3, can run `prepare` in place instead.
4. **Secrets** are re-provisioned through the secret files, never through data (R2-MIG-03).

#### 8.14.2 Forward migration (`lisa core migrate run`)

- **Kept:** every identifier; explicit corrections as owner authority; preferences; source cursors; calendar occurrences; brief and plan history; reminder state; delivery attempts and unknown outcomes; device registrations (as legacy registrations that can be upgraded); capture request keys.
- **Authority of migrated values:**
  - `ownerCorrected` fields become owner authority.
  - Other fields of extracted tasks keep model authority, so R1's extraction behavior is preserved.
  - Tasks created by the owner are owner authority throughout.
  - **Notes carry no flag in R1, and the model writes them only at creation.** An extracted task's notes may therefore be model text or owner text. They become one note piece of **ambiguous authority**: protected like an owner value, never replaced by model or source writes, and displayed "from R1, author unknown".
  - **Notes of any length** migrate losslessly: a note over its value limit becomes a blob, and one over 1 MiB a blob list whose whole-value digest is checked (§8.3.3). `migrate inspect` reports the longest note and the number of blob lists.
  - Idea fields are owner authority, except the planner's unschedulable fields, which are system authority.
- **Files outside SQLite:** `runtime-state.json` becomes Core's schedules, so losing it would only make sources run early. `model-requests.json` becomes the current day's budget. Dry-run captures are archived.
- **Deliveries, in R1's states:**
  - `outcomeUnknown`, and `inProgress` in the submitting phase, stay unknown and are never resent.
  - `inProgress` in the preparing phase returns to pending.
  - Pending deliveries past validity expire.
  - Core starts with delivery suspended.
- **Reminders and intents** map to occurrences and firings (§8.10.1).
- **R1 ledger records** become **outcome-only parents** (origin path `migrated`): scope, request ID, R1's fingerprint, status and expiry, with an unknown child set until verified bytes attach (§8.12.2). Error bodies are re-rendered from R1's templates, so no caller text survives; success bodies are kept until expiry only if R1's audit shows no deletion after they were recorded.
- **Causal metadata** starts from a documented migration origin. No "latest" timestamp is invented to make migrated values win (R2-MIG-02).

#### 8.14.3 Classifying R1 queue entries (finding 2; approval blocker 4)

**Required:** keep each queued action's input and request identity; decide whether it was applied; convert only known semantics; quarantine ambiguity; never replay blindly (R2-MIG-03). **Owner requirement (2026-11-22):** preserve original inputs for deliberate review whenever the available evidence can't establish an effect.

**What R1's code allows us to conclude (§1.6;** `APIRouter.swift`, `WorkHandlers.swift`, `OfflineQueue.swift`, `CaptureService.swift`**)**
- A refusal can coexist with committed children: task and idea PATCH, preferences PATCH and notification actions commit before a later refusal.
- **A recorded answer describes only the attempt that produced it.** A first attempt can commit a correction and lose its answer (a crash or a failed ledger write before recording, or a 504). A retry with the same request ID then meets the revision check, and the resulting 409 is recorded under the same key. That 409 says nothing about the first attempt.
- `attempts` counts attempts that never left the device, and `lastError` keeps only the latest answer. An entry that got a 504 (maybe applied, no uncertainty set) and then a 401 shows only the 401. `uncertainSince` is set once and cleared only by a requeue.
- A 503 can mean "the same request is still being processed", so the request may have been applied.
- **A requeue** assigns a new request ID and resets attempts, errors and uncertainty. Only the original `id` and the current `requestID` survive; every intermediate request ID, and whatever happened under it, is lost.
- **A capture key proves only that the (caller, request ID) pair was used.** Once the ledger record is gone, R1 answers a capture retry from the key whatever the body, so the queued body may differ from what was captured.
- **A capture's title takes effect only sometimes.** For kinds text, link and Apple News, R1 names the news item with the supplied title only when the capture creates the item; a capture of a URL already saved links to the existing item, whose title stays. The item's evidence records which capture created it, and shared-content items are never pruned (§1.6).
- Ledger records expire after 30 days and are written after the mutation.
- Captures were not deletable in R1 production, but R2 can delete them. Audit events, including `data.deleted`, age out after 365 days.

**Evidence, scoped to the original caller, request and child**
- **The file:** status, attempts, `uncertainSince`, last status and message, kept response, `id`, `requestID` and the original body.
- **Core's read-only lookups** for both `requestID` and `id` in the caller's scope (authorized by a lookup grant or the legacy link, §8.14.4–§8.14.5):
  - the migrated R1 record (an outcome-only parent, §8.12.2) or a v1 parent, with fingerprint match, status and attribution: pre-commit, after a commit, or unknown;
  - for captures, the capture key in live records or in tombstones, and Core's **verdict over every effect-bearing input of the capture's kind**:
    - kind `idea`: kind, trimmed text, trimmed URL and channel. R1 stores the idea as `text ?? url` and ignores a title;
    - kinds `text`, `link` and `sharedAppleNews`: kind, trimmed text, trimmed URL and channel, **and the title**;
    - **`matches`** — every input agrees, and for the news kinds either the queued body has no title, or the capture **created** its news item (the item's evidence names this capture) and the item's title equals the title R1 would derive from the queued body (`title`, else the first 120 characters of the text, else the URL, else "Shared item");
    - **`differs`** — an input disagrees, including a news item this capture created under another title: R1 received another body under this key;
    - **`unknown`** — the news item already existed when this capture linked to it and the queued body has a title, so that title had no visible effect and the stored state can't show which body R1 received; or the capture or its item was deleted (a tombstone keeps no content).

    Core compares and returns only the verdict;
  - for captures with unresolved history, how many captures by the same caller match the queued content (a count, never content). It is shown for review and is never a proof;
  - R1's audit horizon, and whether any capture deletion was audited after `createdAt`.
- **Attribution by R1's exact messages:**
  - **Pre-commit:**
    - the 400 body and schema errors;
    - task title, estimate and deadline 400s;
    - "the task changed since it was read" and "the idea changed since it was read";
    - "send between 1 and 50 changes";
    - "unknown action";
    - "the notification does not exist".
  - **After a commit:**
    - "the task cannot move from …" and "the idea cannot move from …";
    - notification-action errors after the receipt;
    - any refusal of a preference change other than the first.
  - **Unknown:** "the item changed since it was read; reload it and try again", and a 404 from task or idea services.

**History, decided before anything else**

| History | Condition |
|---|---|
| `unsent` | status `pending`, `attempts` 0, `requestID = id` |
| `single` | exactly one attempt, `requestID = id`, no `uncertainSince` |
| `unresolved` | anything else: `uncertainSince` set; `requestID ≠ id` (requeued); two or more attempts; status `sending` |

**Classes**

| Class | Meaning | What the owner can safely do |
|---|---|---|
| A. Provably unsent | Positive evidence that no attempt reached R1 or applied | Converted, with the conversion authority (§8.14.4–§8.14.5), if its semantics are known and its expected revision still matches; otherwise offered prefilled for confirmation |
| B. Fully applied | Positive evidence of a 2xx for this caller, request and fingerprint, or the capture key with the verdict `matches` | Nothing; shown in history |
| C1. Rejected, no effect | A definite refusal attributed to before any commit, or from a single-step route, **on an entry whose only attempt produced it** | Re-enter deliberately from a prefilled form against current state; no duplicate is possible |
| C2. Rejected, partial or unknown effect | A refusal after a commit, of unknown attribution, or on an entry with unresolved history | Child by child — each field, or each preference change — the requested value beside the current one; the owner re-applies only what is missing |
| D. Indeterminate | Anything else | The original bytes are kept for inspection; "apply anyway" warns of possible duplication; or discard |

**Rules, by precedence** (a rule applies only if no earlier rule did):
1. **Identity.** A server record for `requestID` or `id` with another fingerprint, or a capture key whose verdict is `differs` or `unknown` → **D**: a collision or an ambiguity, never an application of this input. The review states which input couldn't be established ("your title 'Plumber notes' isn't shown: this link was already saved as 'Home repairs'").
2. **Positive application.** A 2xx server record with a matching fingerprint for `requestID` or `id`, a capture key whose verdict is `matches`, or status `done` with its 2xx response → **B**. With unresolved history on a route that has no idempotency of its own (notification and reminder actions), the history notes "an earlier attempt may also have applied this", read from R1's data.
3. **Receipts.** Notification receipts → **A**: applying one twice is harmless.
4. **Unresolved history outranks everything below, and never yields A or C1:**
   - a server refusal record for `requestID` or `id` → **C2**, labeled "an earlier attempt may have applied part of this";
   - otherwise → **D**. For a capture, the review shows the matching-content count ("a capture with this text exists"), so a capture made under a lost intermediate request ID is never created again automatically.
5. **One attempt with a server refusal record** → **C1** for single-step routes or pre-commit attribution; **C2** otherwise.
6. **One attempt, no server record:** the last answer was 401 or 429 (both answered before R1's ledger), or one of R1's fixed "never sent" descriptions (not paired, credential unavailable, connection refused, host unreachable), and, for a capture, no capture deletion was audited after `createdAt` with the audit horizon at or before it → **A**. Any other answer (422, 500, 502, 503, 504, transport, decoding) → **D**.
7. **Unsent** → **A**, with the same capture condition as rule 6; otherwise **D**.
8. Everything else → **D**.

Wherever these rules can't establish a child's result from reliable, scoped evidence, its original input is preserved for the owner's deliberate review; nothing is converted or discarded on a guess.

**Restart safety:** the import keeps one record per entry, keyed by its manifest index, `id` and `requestID`, with the stored evidence and class. A conversion goes through the parent `(scope, requestID)` (§8.12.2), so a re-run never converts twice (§8.14.4). Class D bytes are kept exactly.

**Acceptance (M9; S-02):** frozen R1 queue files and R1 datasets built from `491c90a` will cover every rule. Among them:
- **an earlier committed correction followed by a recorded 409:** attempt 1 commits the title correction and R1 is killed before recording; attempt 2 records "the task changed since it was read". Expected: C2 ("an earlier attempt may have applied part of this"), with the title already equal to the requested value; never C1;
- **a capture created under a missing intermediate requeue ID:** `id` r0 fails after its retry period; a requeue gives r1, which R1 applies while the answer is lost; the entry fails again; a requeue gives r2; then the import. Expected: D with a matching-content count of 1; never A, and no second capture;
- **an existing capture key with a different queued body and an expired ledger:** the key exists for this caller and request ID, the ledger record has expired, and the stored capture's text differs from the queued body. Expected: D (`differs`), with the queued input kept;
- **two otherwise identical captures with different titles:** under one request ID, R1 kept the first body (title "Home repairs") and created its news item; after the ledger expired, the queued entry carries the same key, text, URL and channel with the title "Plumber notes". Expected: D (`differs`), the queued bytes kept. Under two request IDs with the same text and URL, the first creates the item; the second links to it: the first is B, the second D (`unknown`), because its title had no visible effect;
- **the shared-URL case:** the URL was saved earlier by another capture with another title; the queued link capture has its own key and a title. Expected: D (`unknown`), its bytes kept; the same capture without a title is B;
- an idea capture whose body has a title: B when kind, text, URL and channel match (R1 ignores an idea's title);
- a task PATCH whose correction committed and whose status was refused on a single attempt (C2, with the field diff);
- a capture missing together with its record and deletion audit after a test-path deletion and audit ageing (D, not A);
- a 504 followed by a 401 (unresolved history, D, not A);
- a lost 409 recorded on the server for a plan-block action, on a single attempt (C1);
- a raw `sending` entry with and without a server record (D, or B and C2);
- a restart halfway through the import, with nothing converted twice.

Post-downgrade reconciliation cases are also part of S-02 (§8.14.8).

#### 8.14.4 Upgrading the Mac (finding 3)

**What the old Mac is (§1.6):** an unsandboxed app that embeds the host in process as the local owner, or is a loopback owner client of `lisa serve`. Its queue at `~/Library/Application Support/org.example.lisa.mac/Client/queue/queue.json` holds owner-scope entries only when sends failed. It has no pending-actions screen.

**Upgrade procedure (`docs/MIGRATION-R1-R2.md`):**
1. Stop R1 (§8.14.1).
2. Migrate the data directory.
3. Install the R2 Mac app under the same bundle ID; it runs sandboxed, so the old path is outside its container.
4. Enroll with pairing text.
5. Import the old queue as described below.

**Owner-authorized, read-only import, with separate grants**
1. **Select:** Settings › "Import changes from the previous Lisa for Mac" opens an open panel aimed at the old `Client/queue/` folder. The app opens the file read-only, copies it into its import area, records its SHA-256, and keeps only a read-only security-scoped bookmark. The original is checked unchanged after the import.
2. **Manifest:** `{file SHA-256, entries: [(index, id, requestID, kind, method, path, R1 fingerprint)]}`, signed by the Mac and uploaded with an `owner-import` challenge (`POST /v2/migration/imports`). Core stores it as pending and returns an import ID; the Mac shows the manifest digest as eight words.
3. **Lookup grant:** `lisa core migrate import approve-lookups <id> --digest <words>` on the administrative terminal, signed and bound to the import ID, the Mac's device ID, the manifest digest and scope `owner`. It must be used within 10 minutes, then stays valid until the import completes or for 7 days. It lets Core run the read-only lookups of §8.14.3 for exactly the manifest's `requestID` and `id` values. **It authorizes nothing else**, and in particular no conversion.
4. **Items:** the Mac uploads each entry as a **legacy import item**: the entry's fields and original body bytes, signed by the Mac (`POST /v2/migration/imports/{id}/items`).
   - An item's ID is derived from the import ID and its manifest index, so a re-upload is a duplicate (same bytes) or `identity_reuse` (different bytes).
   - Core recomputes each item's R1 fingerprint from its bytes. An item whose fingerprint differs from its manifest line is refused as malformed.
   - Items may arrive in several parts and in any order.
5. **Classification at Core.** Core classifies each item (§8.14.3) from its file evidence and Core's own lookups, and stores the result with the evidence. Later reads return the stored result, so retention running in between changes nothing. The Mac receives `{class, evidence summary}`, never a body, title or value from Core's records.
