6. **Review and conversion approval.** `lisa core migrate import review <id>` shows each entry's class, evidence and requested values. `lisa core migrate import approve-conversion <id> --entries <indexes | class-A> --digest <words>` records the owner's signed approval, bound to the import ID, the manifest digest and the listed entries' indexes and fingerprints. This approval is the conversion authority (§8.12.2).
7. **Conversion,** in manifest order: entry i is converted only after every earlier entry has an outcome (converted, or classified and not approved). Each conversion is one transaction through `LisaLegacyRequests`, keyed by `(owner, requestID)`:
   - **no parent:** Core creates the parent (origin path `import`, authority the approval) with its complete child set, the children and their receipts, and records the item's outcome;
   - **a parent with the same fingerprint** (a v1 retry, or a migrated R1 record, got there first): nothing new is created; the item's outcome references that parent and its recorded outcome;
   - **a parent with another fingerprint:** class D (collision); nothing is converted.

   Classes B, C1, C2 and D are never converted automatically. The owner re-enters C1 and C2 values deliberately from the review, and applies, edits or discards D entries in the app's inspection list.
8. **Owner-only and administrative keys.** R1 lets only the owner on the host change some keys, and R2-TRUST-05 keeps administrative keys CLI-only. An approved preferences request that reaches an administrative key waits there: that child is `held_for_administrative_review`, every later child is `pending_behind_review`, and earlier children are converted (§8.12.2). The owner decides at the administrative terminal with `lisa core legacy resolve owner <requestID> apply|refuse`: `apply` continues the request in order; `refuse` ends it as R1 ends a refused change, with the later children `not_reached`. The item reports `awaiting_administrative_review` meanwhile, and the decision is recorded on the parent, which then completes. Discarding the entry in review before conversion creates nothing.
9. **Resumption.** The Mac records, per entry, that it was uploaded and the item receipt Core signed (class, outcome, parent). After a crash it reads `GET /v2/migration/imports/{id}` and continues. Core's records — manifest, grants, stored evidence, item outcomes and parents — make every step idempotent.

**Recovery facts (import).** *Survives:* the Mac's import record and its copy of the queue file; Core's import record, items, evidence and parents; the unchanged original file. *Who acts:* the owner approves lookups and conversions and resolves held children; Core converts; the Mac uploads and displays. *Safe after an interruption:* re-upload and re-read; the parent key makes a second conversion impossible. *What the owner sees when the outcome is unknown:* the entry as class D, with its reason ("an earlier attempt may have saved this") and its original content, to apply, edit or discard.

**Acceptance (M9 harness; X7; S-03):**
- **the full input range:** a preferences request of 50 valid changes — all to one key, and again spread over three keys with repeats — converted, then relayed to a second replica in reverse order. Every child will have at most one dependency, the replica will apply each key's chain in request order, and the final values will equal R1's;
- a multi-preference request (three changes: one key repeated, one administrative key in the middle), a task PATCH and a capture, imported **in parts and out of order** (items 3, 1, 2): conversion waits for manifest order;
- **the held middle child, in every history:** import first, then a v1 retry through the owner listener; v1 first, then the import; each with `apply` and with `refuse`; and an import entry discarded in review, then converted by a v1 retry. While the parent waits, the frozen queue's retry will get the 503 and stay pending, and the frozen CLI will report R1's "unavailable"; after the decision every retry will get the recorded answer. For every child in every history, its authority (approval, link, R1 credential or the CLI decision), disposition, effect on the projection and Core-signed receipt will be compared: the same IDs and digests everywhere, and the same dispositions for the same decision;
- **crash and retry around the hold:** a kill after the parent and its prefix commit, and another after the decision commits but before the suffix's last child; each restart resumes, and nothing is converted twice;
- **an outcome-only migrated parent:** an R1 record of a three-change preferences request answered 409. A v1 retry with the same bytes will get R1's recorded, sanitized answer; an import item with the same bytes will attach them, and review will show each change's R1 history as `unknown_in_r1` beside the current values; an item with other bytes under that key will be class D and attach nothing;
- **a restricted sibling:** a preferences request changing `model.endpoint` (a synthetic canary URL) between two permitted keys. The actual bytes a relay forwards — change-feed pages, nearby frames, receipts — will be captured and searched: neither the canary, the key name nor R1's fingerprint will appear; the relay will verify membership, order and the redacted receipt; and the replication allowlist check will pass;
- **an interruption after the first durable step:** a kill after the manifest is stored, and another after item 1's conversion commits but before the Mac records it. Each restart resumes, and nothing is converted twice;
- **a partially committed prefix:** an entry whose migrated R1 record shows a later preference change refused after earlier ones committed. The migrated parent is found, nothing is converted, and the class is C2 with the per-change values;
- **a different body declaring the same fingerprint:** an item whose bytes don't match its manifest fingerprint is refused as malformed; an item with another body under an existing parent's request ID is class D;
- **for every child:** its disposition, its effect on the projection, and its Core-signed receipt with its own digest (or its redacted receipt). A simulated relay forwards the children to a second replica in reverse order and checks signatures, ID derivation, `setDigest` and same-entity chains: no child is applied before its predecessor, and none is missing from the set;
- an expired or mismatched grant refused; a lookup outside the manifest refused; the lookup grant shown to authorize no conversion;
- the original file's hash unchanged.

**X7 prerequisite:** a genuine old Mac app.
- The owner builds R1's Mac app from the reconstructed `491c90a` tree (§8.18) at or before X3 and records the Xcode version.
- If it doesn't build, X7 uses a fixture queue file produced by the frozen Linux R1 client and placed at the old path, and reports the genuine-app part as `NOT_RUN_ENVIRONMENT` (owner; the R1 Mac app doesn't build).

#### 8.14.5 Upgrading a paired iPhone or iPad

**What the old companion supports (§1.6):** only `/v1/` with API major version 1 and R1's ten error codes; pairing by code; a Keychain identity `{installationID, deviceID, credential}`; direct push registration; trust of up to eight owner anchors plus an optional certificate pin.
- After migration it reaches Core only if the owner changes its host address and trust settings to Core's. Its migrated legacy registration then authenticates it.
- Queued mutations go through the v1 translation (§8.12.2).

**Linking the old registration to the new identity**
- On first launch, the R2 app (same bundle ID, same container and Keychain access) reads the R1 identity item and queue file read-only, and computes the queue manifest as the Mac does (§8.14.4).
- Its enrollment request includes `legacyLink {r1DeviceID, r1InstallationID, r1Credential, queueManifestDigest}`. These bytes are secret-bearing and stored like the rest of the request (§8.1.2).
- Core verifies the credential in constant time against the legacy registration's verifier, and checks that the registration is active and unlinked.
- In the consuming transaction (§8.1.2 step 5), Core:
  - records the link with the declared manifest digest;
  - moves eligible device-local settings (notification preferences and categories) to the new device;
  - maps delivery history to it;
  - removes the legacy registration from routing;
  - retires the legacy credential (verifier deleted, state `upgraded`).

  The old registration's push token is deleted when the new device registers its token, and only one of the two is ever eligible.

**Lookups, import and conversion:**
- After linking, the device uploads its manifest and items exactly as the Mac does (§8.14.4), in scope `device:<legacy ID>`, with a `legacy-link-lookup` challenge. Core classifies them (§8.14.3).
- **Authority.** The link proves the R1 credential under which R1 would have applied this device's requests. It therefore authorizes the lookups in that scope and the conversion of class A entries of the declared manifest — no other entries and no other scope. It is not derived from a lookup grant.
- Changes R1 refuses from a device (owner-only keys, deletes) are decided as R1 decides them: `refused (403)`. The app shows them with "Only your administrative terminal can change this setting."
- Classes B, C1, C2 and D are reviewed in the app, where the owner re-enters or discards each one deliberately.
- If the R1 credential no longer verifies (rotated or revoked), enrollment proceeds without a link, and the import needs the owner's lookup grant and conversion approval, like the Mac's, bound to scope `device:<id>`.

**What a frozen Linux client can prove:** R1's `LisaClient` and offline queue, built from `491c90a` on Linux, prove:
- wire compatibility: version header, error decoding, status mapping;
- queue state transitions and the file format;
- the link and lookup flows with an R1 credential.

They cannot prove Apple presentation, Keychain access, the sandbox, URLSession trust, or the update of the installed app on a device. X7 covers those.

**Acceptance (M9; X7; S-03):** the checks will cover:
- link and import with a valid credential: class A entries converted under the link, an owner-only key refused as R1 refuses it, and other classes left for review;
- an item outside the declared manifest refused;
- refusal with a revoked one, and the import then waiting for the owner's grants;
- no device eligible twice at any point (routing checked before and after the link commit);
- delivery history and preferences preserved;
- the old credential refused afterwards;
- on devices at X7: the R1 companion upgraded in place.

#### 8.14.6 Cutover

The R1 host never resumes automatically. The Mac connects as an R2 client. Devices reconcile before normal scheduling starts. The owner reviews, then resumes delivery (R2-MIG-04).

#### 8.14.7 Rollback (finding 13)

**Required:** a compatible pre-migration backup or an explicit reverse migration; never old software on a newer schema; never two schedulers (R2-MIG-04).

1. **Preflight** (`lisa core migrate rollback --preflight`, listener mode) creates **rollback generation R**: a signed record with a counter and a random ID. It is published in capabilities and `GET /v2/rollback`, and lists everything outstanding.
2. **Acknowledgements.** Each device sends a signed `{R, deviceID, incarnation, finalJournalPosition, outstanding {signedUncommitted, waiting, drafts, quarantined, unresolvedResolutions}, exportDigest?, headSeen}`.
   - It covers everything up to `finalJournalPosition`: signed operations without a Core receipt (waiting ones included), drafts, quarantined inputs (R1 queue items and rejected work kept for inspection), and draft conflict resolutions not yet submitted.
   - The acknowledgement is clean only if every count is zero after a final sync. Otherwise it must carry the digest of a protected export containing everything it counts.
   - **Silence is never read as clean:** a device that hasn't acknowledged is listed as outstanding with the last position and pending count it reported.
3. **After acknowledging,** a device keeps saving, because capture is never refused.
   - Entries beyond the acknowledged position are **late**. The device shows "Saved on this device. Lisa Core is being rolled back; export this change." and offers export.
   - A late entry is never shown as synced, and Core never learns of it: only the device and its exports hold it. After Core stops, the app says "Lisa Core was rolled back; export your device-only changes".
4. **Fence** (`--fence`, owner, after preflight review):
   - Core intake is fenced: v2 gets `rollback_in_progress`; v1 gets 503 `unavailable`.
   - Connectors and the scheduler stop. The outbox is suspended.
   - In-flight attempts settle: Core waits until no delivery is `inProgress`, and a `submitting` attempt that times out becomes `outcomeUnknown`.
   - Cancel commands go to every delegated assignment. Confirmations are collected until the owner proceeds, and unconfirmed slots are recorded "may fire locally".
   - Then the **rollback archive** is written: the v5 backup plus a rollback record. The record holds the signed acknowledgements; each outstanding device with the last position and pending count it reported; waiting operations; unresolved conflicts; uncertain and held deliveries; reservations; **Core's final disposition and digest for every operation it decided**; and the **projection map** from each R2 entity to the R1 record reverse migration writes for it, with that record's ID, revision and value (§8.14.8).
5. **Reverse migration** (`--reverse`, offline; §8.14.8) produces the v4 database and regenerates the two sidecars:
   - `runtime-state.json`: version; jobs with next run, consecutive failures, last run and last outcome; last cycle; last maintenance — all from Core's schedules;
   - `model-requests.json`: version, today's day in the owner's zone, and the count.
6. **Verification on a disposable copy** (`migrate verify-r1 --copy`): the result is copied, and the frozen R1 binary runs `status` and `run-once` with dry-run delivery against the copy only; the frozen R1 client, queue and CLI then replay request IDs from the reversed ledger against it (§8.14.8). Opening an R1 host runs recovery and writes, so the real directory is never opened during verification.
7. **The owner starts R1.** R2 never runs a scheduler again on that dataset.

**What the rollback report claims.** Only what each device acknowledged or reported: "iPhone: acknowledged through position 420 at <time>; nothing outstanding", or "iPad: no acknowledgement; last reported position 512 with 2 pending, at <time>". Work a device saved after its acknowledgement, or after its last report, is **unknown to Core**. The report never counts or estimates it and says: "Changes saved on a device after the time shown aren't known to Lisa Core. They can be recovered from that device's export." Unreachable devices stay visible in the report, and their work is recoverable later through their exports and the post-downgrade tool.

#### 8.14.8 Reverse migration rules and the post-downgrade archive tool

**v5 to v4**
- R2 tables are dropped after being archived. Projections keep R1's record shape; R2 only added optional fields.
- Note pieces become R1's `notes` text in a deterministic order. A blob list is concatenated and checked against its whole-value digest, so a migrated note of any length returns byte for byte (§8.3.3).
- **Fields with several values,** which v4 can't hold:
  - the v4 record keeps the owner-authored value;
  - when owner values conflict, it keeps the one the owner selects in preflight, or otherwise R1's last value;
  - every alternative goes into the report;
  - owner or ambiguous-authority text is never replaced by a model or source value.
- `ownerCorrected` is regenerated from authority.
- **Deliveries:**
  - attempted ones keep their final state;
  - ones R2 created but never attempted become `cancelled` "rollback: not attempted";
  - slots that may fire locally have their R1 deliveries to that device suppressed;
  - uncertain and held deliveries become `outcomeUnknown`.
- **The ledger.** Unexpired v1 parents become R1 ledger records, each with R2's remaining expiry. A success body that is still valid is written as recorded. An invalidated body is written as the route's **terminal reply** (§8.12.2), and an error as its sanitized template — never `{}` — so every frozen R1 caller decodes the answer and R1 never runs the request again. An outcome-only parent writes R1's recorded answer back, re-rendered if it is an error.
- Legacy registrations are restored. R2-only devices are listed so the owner can pair them in R1 if wanted.
- **Fallback:** the rollback backup from `prepare`. Owner edits made after migration are then listed for re-entry, and R2's delivery attempts are imported as final states.
- **Limits once R1's own ledger expires** (stated in the rollback report). R1 then forgets those request IDs:
  - a capture retry is still answered from R1's permanent capture key while the capture exists. A capture deleted in R2 isn't in v4, so a lingering R1 queue entry could create it again — unless R1's queue has already failed it after its 30-day retry period, which equals the shortest retention;
  - a task or idea PATCH retry meets R1's revision check;
  - a preferences PATCH retry applies its changes again;
  - task creation is never queued by R1 (§1.6).

**The post-downgrade tool: `lisa archive`** (part of the R2 `lisa` binary)
- **Runs in files mode:** it never starts Core, never runs a scheduler, and never opens an R2 database for writing. It keeps its own **reconcile store**, a small SQLite file in a working directory the owner names, opened under an exclusive lock. It reads R1's database only read-only (`mode=ro`), in one read transaction per proof.
- **Inputs:** the rollback archive, device archives, and the owner's key backup directory (current and retired export keys, signing-key history).
- **What each key does.** An archive's payload key is sealed to Core's export key (current Kx2, or a retired one, from the key backup) and to the device's own export key: Kx2 only **decrypts** it. Its contents are **authenticated** by the device's signature, checked against that device's key in a trusted roster — the rollback archive's Core-signed roster, verified through the signing-key history in the key backup. An entry that decrypts but doesn't authenticate is listed and never executed.
- **Logical entry identity,** fixed before anything is shown:
  - a signed operation: `(dataset, operationID, digest)`. The same operation in two archives — overlapping exports from one device, or a device export and the rollback archive — is one entry. The same ID with another digest is shown as an identity conflict, and neither copy is executed;
  - a draft: `(dataset, device, draftID, draftRevision)`; different revisions of one draft are shown together for the owner to choose between;
  - quarantined R1 bytes: `(dataset, device, queue id, requestID)`.
- **Eligibility, one rule** (`LisaArchive.eligibility`), used identically by `inspect`, `reconcile` and Core's `import-journal` (§8.6.6): an entry is eligible if its origin is active in the trusted roster and not revoked there or by `lisa archive revoke-device` (which the owner uses for a device lost or stolen since the rollback); or, if its origin is revoked, if it carries a Core receipt on the same lineage whose commit position precedes the revocation (the pre-revocation receipt exception, §8.5.4). A device-supplied time or the device's own older roster never establishes eligibility. Ineligible entries stay listed, and the owner can only re-enter their content deliberately as new owner actions.
- **`inspect`** lists every entry with its verification (dataset, signature, the key it was sealed to), its eligibility, its **rollback-cut disposition** (below), and the matching R1 record, read through R1's running owner listener with read-only GETs, or from the read-only database snapshot.
- **The rollback cut is consulted before the first send of any entry.** The tool looks up the operation's ID among the rollback archive's dispositions:
  - **decided `accepted` (or `accepted_with_conflict`) with the same digest:** Core applied it before the fence, and its effect is already in the reversed v4 database, even if the device never received the receipt. The entry becomes `applied_before_rollback` and **nothing is sent**. The entity mapping is seeded from the projection map (R2 entity → R1 record ID), so a dependent offline edit goes to the existing R1 record;
  - **the same ID with another digest:** an identity conflict; neither copy is executed;
  - **decided `rejected`:** shown with Core's reason; re-entered only deliberately;
  - **absent:** Core never decided it; the entry is a reconciliation candidate.
- **`reconcile`** asks the owner about each eligible candidate, in dependency order, and records every step in the store **before** acting:
  1. **Intent:** the entry, the attempt number, the R1 route, the exact body bytes, the R1 request ID derived from the entry identity and attempt (`lisa-rc-` followed by 26 base32 characters), what R1 showed before (record ID, revision and value, or "absent"), and the time. Committed before anything is sent.
  2. **Sending:** a `sending` mark, committed immediately before the first request byte.
  3. **Send** through R1's owner listener.
  4. **Completion:** R1's status and answer, the resulting R1 record and revision, the **entity mapping**, and a post-read of the record. An entry that edits something an earlier entry created uses the mapping, and waits while it is missing.
  5. **An intent without a completion** (R1 or the tool was killed, or the answer was lost) is resolved by its route's proof below **before any further attempt**, or shown as indeterminate.
- **Proof of the outcome, per route** (R1 facts, §1.6):

  | Change | R1 route | Outcome after a lost answer |
  |---|---|---|
  | Create an idea | `POST /v1/captures` with kind `idea`, R1's only way to create one; then `PATCH /v1/ideas/{id}` for its other fields, through the mapping | **Safe resend.** R1 deduplicates captures permanently by caller and request ID and never prunes or (in production) deletes them, so resending the same bytes under the same request ID returns the existing capture with its idea, or creates it once |
  | Capture text or a link | `POST /v1/captures` | The same |
  | Create a task | `POST /v1/tasks` | **Not sent** (no `sending` mark): send it. **Applied** only if a read-only snapshot of R1's database holds R1's ledger record for `owner:<request ID>` with the intent's fingerprint; its body names the task, and the mapping is recorded. The request ID is unique to this intent, so that record can exist only in the database that processed the request. **Anything else is indeterminate:** R1 writes the record after its commit and prunes it after its retention, and no other R1 record identifies the request, so neither a time window nor a listing proves the create didn't happen. The owner sees every task in the same snapshot whose content equals the intent's (title, notes, priority, deadline, estimate, splittable), with R1's creation times labeled as R1's clock, and chooses: use one (the mapping is recorded), send again as a new attempt (warned that it may duplicate), or skip |
  | Field edit or status change of a task or idea | `PATCH` with the recorded `expectedRevision` | Read the record. Revision unchanged → not applied, resend. Moved, with every intended value present → applied. Anything else (a partial correction, or someone else's change) → indeterminate, with the values side by side |
  | Preference set, confirm or delete | `PATCH /v1/preferences`, one change per request | The rules below |

- **Preferences, each kind by its own rule.** Each change is compared with the key's record at the rollback cut (record ID, revision, value, origin, confirmed) from the projection map, and with R1's current record, read just before sending and again just after.
  - **Set** to value v:
    - the current record is the cut's record at the cut's revision → send with that `expectedRevision`; R1 refuses it if the record changed in between;
    - absent at the cut and absent now (the entry created the key) → send without a revision. R1 can't guard an absent key, so a key created in between would be overwritten; the post-read reports any difference;
    - present at the cut and absent now (deleted after the downgrade) → **indeterminate**: R1 would insert the key despite any revision, recreating what someone deleted;
    - another record ID (deleted and recreated) or another revision → indeterminate, with the values side by side;
    - the current record already holds v, explicit and confirmed → **satisfied, provenance unknown**: nothing is sent, and the log says "already had this value; who set it is unknown", never "applied".
  - **Confirm** of an inferred value v (R1's confirm checks no revision and needs only the key):
    - the current record is the cut's, still inferred and unconfirmed, holding v → send; the post-read checks that the record ID and value didn't change in between, and otherwise reports "a different value may have been confirmed";
    - already confirmed with v → satisfied, provenance unknown;
    - another value, another record ID, or absent → indeterminate, or nothing to confirm; never sent.
  - **Delete** (R1 refuses deletes from devices, so the tool sends one only as the owner's own change, after the owner confirms it):
    - the current record is the cut's at the cut's revision → send; the post-read confirms it is gone;
    - absent → satisfied, provenance unknown ("already absent; who deleted it is unknown");
    - recreated or changed → indeterminate; never sent.
  - **Residual:** R1 has no conditional confirm or delete, so a change made between the tool's read and R1's commit can be overwritten; the pre- and post-reads detect and report it.
- **The owner-visible log:** `lisa archive log` prints every decision, intent, R1 answer, proof and mapping, with times. A rerun of `reconcile` skips completed entries and resolves open intents through the proofs above.

**Recovery facts (rollback and reconciliation).** *Survives:* the rollback archive (dispositions and projection map), device archives, the key backup, the reconcile store and R1's own database. *Who acts:* the owner approves every entry, maps indeterminate creates and records lost devices; the tool proves outcomes from R1's own records. *Safe after an interruption:* rerun `reconcile`; an intent is never resent without its route's proof. *What the owner sees when the outcome is unknown:* "Entry 421 (create task 'Call the plumber'): R1 may have created it; R1's record of this request is gone or was never written. One task with exactly this content exists (task <id>, created <time by R1's clock>). Use this task, send again (may duplicate), or skip."

**Acceptance (M9; S-02, S-13; narrative §19.2):**
- a save after acknowledgement; a device that never acknowledges (listed, never read as clean); an acknowledgement that counts a quarantined item and a draft resolution, which isn't clean until the export covers them; **the rollback report naming only acknowledged and reported positions**, with the iPhone's later saves absent from it;
- a sandboxed Mac export outside the container (X3, X7);
- loss of the originating installation; an export-key rotation before the export;
- an interrupted reverse migration resumed with no partial state; a 3 MiB migrated note returned byte for byte;
- provider acceptance across the cutover: accepted before stays accepted, and accepted by R2 arrives as accepted;
- conflicting values that v4 can't hold, reported with owner text kept;
- the frozen R1 `run-once` on the copy sending nothing again;
- **the reversed ledger with the frozen callers:** on the verification copy, the frozen R1 CLI and typed client and the frozen queue replay request IDs whose bodies R2 invalidated — a capture deleted in R2, a task created and then deleted, a preferences answer that listed a deleted key — and an error carrying a canary. Every answer will decode, none will contain deleted content or the canary, and the database diff will show no mutation;
- **reconciliation (S-02):**
  - **the rollback cut first:** the iPad's create of task T3 was committed by Core before the fence, and its receipt was lost; its offline title edit of T3 was never sent. Both arrive in the iPad's export. The create will be marked `applied_before_rollback` and never sent; the mapping from T3 to its R1 record will be seeded from the projection map; the edit will go to that record with its revision; R1 will hold one T3, with the new title;
  - **overlapping exports:** two iPhone archives holding the same operations, and an iPad archive holding an operation also present in the rollback archive: each reconciled or skipped once, with every archive named in the log;
  - **a task create with both processes killed after R1's commit,** in four variants: R1's ledger record written (applied; mapping recorded; no second task); R1 killed before writing it (indeterminate; the snapshot's equal-content candidate shown; the owner maps it); **R1's clock set back one day** between the attempt and the proof (the same results, because no rule reads a clock); **past R1's retention**, with the record pruned (indeterminate, never resent automatically). A variant killed before the `sending` mark sends once. A variant with 120 tasks in R1 (three API pages) shows that candidates come from one snapshot, never from pages;
  - **a restart between intent and completion,** for every route;
  - **preferences:** a set whose key was deleted after the downgrade (indeterminate; nothing inserted); a set whose key was deleted and recreated (indeterminate); a set whose value the owner had already entered in R1 (satisfied, provenance unknown); a confirm whose inferred value changed (never sent); a confirm racing an R1 change between read and send (reported); a delete of an already absent key (satisfied); a delete of a recreated key (never sent);
  - **an idea created through R1's capture path,** with the answer lost: the resend returns the existing capture and idea, and the dependent tag edit uses the mapping;
  - **the revision-checked title edit:** unchanged revision → resent; moved with the value present → applied; moved otherwise → indeterminate;
  - **one eligibility rule everywhere:** an operation from a device revoked with `revoke-device`, carrying an early creation time, is ineligible in `inspect`, `reconcile` and `import-journal` alike; one with a same-lineage pre-revocation receipt is eligible in all three; an ineligible entry is re-entered deliberately as a new owner action;
  - **keys:** an archive that decrypts with Kx2 but whose signature doesn't verify against the trusted roster is listed and never executed;
  - for each case, the resulting R1 rows and IDs, and the log, checked;
- recovery after R1 resumes, using only the rollback archive, the device exports and the documented key backup in a fresh directory.

### 8.15 Limits, SQLite capacity and performance (R2-LIMIT-01–03; protocol limits; SQLite capacity and latency)

#### 8.15.1 Defaults (R2-LIMIT-01), each configurable and checked

| Resource | Default |
|---|---|
| Enrolled devices | 10 |
| Operation body (canonical, metadata included) | 64 KiB (§8.3.3) |
| Operation batch | 100 operations and 512 KiB of signed bytes, whichever comes first |
| Encoded HTTP request body | 768 KiB (§8.16.2) |
| Nearby frame | 1 MiB total |
| Change page, snapshot chunk | 512 KiB |
| Content blob | 1 MiB, in 256 KiB chunks, committed inside SQLite; blob lists only for notes over 1 MiB that came from R1, and edits of them (§8.3.3); 256 MiB of staged blobs per replica; orphans expire after 7 days |
| Paged artifacts | Causal-summary pages 256 KiB; manifest pages 4,096 entries; roster pages 64 KiB; all Merkle-bound (§8.1.3) |
| Dependencies | 32 per operation; 1,000 waiting per origin incarnation; 64 MiB of waiting bodies; per transaction at most 256 edges expanded, 256 entries enqueued and 256 released; cycle walks of 1,024 nodes per step (§8.2.2) |
| Incremental history | 90 days |
| Waiting operations | 14 days |
| Local notifications | 48 hours ahead, 32 requests (§8.10.5) |
| Inference | One in flight, ten a day plus the rolling window, one counted repair (§8.9.2) |
| Legacy children | At most one dependency each (the immediate same-entity predecessor); up to 50 per preferences request, as R1 allows (§8.12.2) |
| Trust bundles | 64 roster pages, 16 key transitions and 4 MiB per bundle; 16 bundles per recovery; three outstanding evidence codes, each valid for 10 minutes (§8.5.3) |
| Provider connectors | R1's message-size limits; calendar windows as R2 specifies (§8.19.2); Graph at most 4 concurrent requests per mailbox; backoff honoring `Retry-After` |
| Retries | Finite per attempt cycle, with persisted next-attempt times |

#### 8.15.2 SQLite capacity: readers, WAL and storage exhaustion

**Required:** storage exhaustion never reports a successful unsaved capture (R2-L33); unsynchronized captures, pending operations, conflicts, uncertain deliveries and replay metadata are never evicted (R2-LIMIT-02).

- **WAL in practice:** `journal_size_limit` only truncates the WAL file after a checkpoint resets it; it does not cap a live WAL. The live WAL grows while any reader holds an old snapshot. It is bounded here by bounding readers and checkpointing:
  - `wal_autocheckpoint` stays on; a passive checkpoint runs after each batch install, and a truncating one after a snapshot swap.
  - Every long reader belongs to Lisa: snapshot generation on Core, staging catch-up passes and export assembly on clients, search queries. Each runs on a read connection registered with a **reader monitor** that knows its start time and purpose.
- **Release before the reserve is threatened.** The store keeps a capture reserve (64 MiB on clients, configurable on Core). The monitor samples free space (`statfs`) and the WAL size every second while readers are active, and acts when either of these holds:
  - free space falls below the reserve plus twice the WAL size;
  - a reader has been open longer than its budget (search 5 s; catch-up pass 2 s; export 60 s; snapshot generation 10 minutes).

  It then interrupts the reader (`sqlite3_interrupt` on its connection), finalizes its statements, ends the read transaction, and runs a truncating checkpoint. The interrupted work is retried later; for snapshot generation, from a new cut.
- **Exhaustion:** `SQLITE_FULL` (or an `ENOSPC` from staging files) aborts only that write's transaction. A capture reports "Not saved: storage is full" with the action needed. Earlier saves are intact, and the replica stays openable.
- **Truthful under bursts faster than the sample.** The one-second sample only decides when to release readers; it never decides whether a save succeeded.
  - Every save's outcome is its own commit result, so a burst that fills the disk between samples still ends in "Not saved" for the write that failed, never in a false "Saved".
  - Writes other than captures (staging, blob promotion, snapshot generation, release steps) read free space with a fresh `statfs` at the start of each bounded transaction and stop before the reserve.
  - **A ballast file** the size of the capture reserve (`reserve.bin` in the replica directory, excluded from backups like the rest) is released only by the capture path: on `SQLITE_FULL` the capture truncates the ballast and retries once. If that succeeds, the capture is saved and the app says "Storage is almost full"; the ballast is restored when space returns. If it fails, the capture reports "Not saved".
- **Measured together** (M2, clients; M3, Core): staged tables, staged indexes, the live WAL and the database file at their peak during an installation, against a reserve sized from those numbers.

**Acceptance (S-17)**
- **M2:** the first real use of the rootless engine (§9) runs the replica harness inside a `swift:6.4.2-trixie` container with the database directory on a `--tmpfs …:size=…` mount, so the kernel itself returns `ENOSPC`. Checks:
  - a pinned reader (a long search) during continuous captures: WAL growth measured, the reader interrupted at its budget or at the space threshold, then the WAL truncated;
  - storage filling during a staged installation: the capture before the reserve saved, the capture that doesn't fit reported unsaved, and the database consistent after restart;
  - **a burst that fills the tmpfs between two samples:** every capture reported "Saved" is present after a restart, every failed one reported "Not saved", and the ballast lets one capture through after other writers filled the disk;
  - `max_page_count` for the in-database case on the Fedora host.
- **M3:** the same on Core's snapshot generation and intake.

The engine, context, architecture and image digests of that first run are recorded (O-10).

#### 8.15.3 Performance (R2-LIMIT-03)

- **Data:** synthetic, with 10,000 searchable records, 100,000 historical operations, three clients and recurring calendars.
- **Targets:** capture p95 within 500 ms; search p95 within 1 second; 100 small operations converging within 5 seconds after an authenticated foreground connection on a healthy link; normal reads never waiting for Core or a model.
- **Capture p95 is overall.** It includes captures that waited on a snapshot swap or a release batch, broken down by cause.
- **Reporting, in separate classes:**
  - **Fedora host** (M2, M9): hardware and method recorded.
  - **Simulated link** (M6): labeled simulated.
  - **Physical devices at X7:** 100 small operations timed over an already authenticated, healthy foreground link between named devices, against the 5-second target. Hardware, OS versions, payload size, dataset size and the actual result are recorded.

### 8.16 Transfer sizes, encoding and streaming (R2-LIMIT-01; protocol limits)

#### 8.16.1 Encoding

- **Signed bodies:** one canonical JSON form. UTF-8 (invalid UTF-8 refused), object keys sorted by Unicode scalar value, no whitespace, integers only (instants in milliseconds, decimals as strings), minimal escaping, no duplicate keys, nesting depth at most 16.
- **Signatures:** raw 64-byte P-256 values over a domain-separated prefix plus the body. Nothing re-encodes a signed body.
- **Over HTTP:** signed bodies travel as base64url inside JSON envelopes.
- **In nearby frames:** raw bytes.

#### 8.16.2 The four budgets, checked separately

| Layer | Unit | Limit | Where checked |
|---|---|---|---|
| Semantic | Canonical operation body, including metadata | 64 KiB | At creation (client) and intake (Core), before decoding the payload |
| Semantic | Batch | ≤ 100 operations and ≤ 512 KiB of bodies plus signatures | Client splits; Core checks |
| HTTP | Encoded request body (JSON envelope with base64url bodies) | 768 KiB, which is ≥ 512 KiB × 4/3 plus 64 KiB of envelope | Server, on the bytes actually received |
| Frame | Nearby frame (8-byte header and payload) | 1 MiB total | From the header, before allocation |

- **Streamed enforcement.** The server counts received body bytes whether or not `Content-Length` is present.
  - With `Content-Length` over the limit, it refuses before reading.
  - Without it (chunked), it reads into a bounded buffer and stops at limit + 1, answering 413 (`payloadTooLarge` for v1, oversized for v2) and closing the connection.
  - A body shorter or longer than its declared `Content-Length` is a framing error.
- **Responses** stream change pages, chunks and blobs without assembling them in memory.
- **UTF-8 and escaping:** limits count encoded bytes, so multi-byte characters and escaped control characters count at their encoded size (§8.3.3).

#### 8.16.3 Blobs and staging

- **Blob-backed values:** a capture text, note or description over its value limit becomes a blob of at most 1 MiB (a migrated note over 1 MiB, a blob list, §8.3.3). The operation carries its digest and length, so the signature covers the content. The operation waits, as a dependency, until the blob is committed.
- **Where committed blobs live: inside SQLite,** on Core and on every replica, in a `blob_chunks` table of 256 KiB rows keyed by blob digest and chunk index. A blob is therefore committed, backed up and restored with the records that reference it, and no file can be orphaned or half-moved.
- **Staging and promotion:**
  - Incoming chunks of 256 KiB are verified by digest and written to staging files, with progress recorded per blob; interrupted staging resumes from the last verified chunk.
  - **Promotion** is its own transaction: once every chunk is verified, the blob's chunks are inserted from the staging file (at most 1 MiB, so the transaction and its WAL growth stay bounded) with reference count zero and a 7-day orphan deadline. The staging file is deleted after the commit; a crash in between leaves a stale staging file that the next start removes, because the committed blob is already complete.
  - **Commit order:** promotion first, then the operation's transaction, which increments the reference count. An operation never references a blob that isn't committed.
  - A committed blob still unreferenced after 7 days expires (an orphan). Staging is bounded at 256 MiB per replica; beyond it, `insufficient_storage` for new blobs.
  - **Backups** need nothing extra: SQLite's online backup copies the blob rows with the records at the same cut (§8.4.4).
- **Core-wide bounds:** storage for blobs, waiting operations and staging is bounded per dataset. Exceeding a bound gives backpressure (`insufficient_storage`) and a health warning, never silent loss.

**What fits where:** every signed unit (operation, batch, roster page, receipt, change page) fits one frame and one HTTP message. Snapshots, artifacts and blobs are paged or chunked, and checked piece by piece. Attachments are still not replicated (R2-DATA-03).

**Flow control:** chunks are 256–512 KiB, within TN3213's advice of at least 64 KiB. Each send waits for buffer space, and each receive is consumed before the next read.

**Acceptance (M1 codec, M3 HTTP, M6 frames; S-16):** the checks will cover:
- exact boundaries: a 64 KiB body accepted and one byte more refused; 100 operations accepted and 101 refused; a 512 KiB batch accepted and one byte more refused; a 768 KiB HTTP body accepted and one byte more refused;
- the same over chunked transfer without `Content-Length`, refused at the byte count and with memory measured;
- a 1 MiB frame accepted, and one byte more closing the session;
- a batch of maximum-size operations whose content needs multi-byte UTF-8 and escaped control characters, with base64 and JSON overhead measured;
- a blob interrupted mid-staging and resumed; a kill between promotion and the referencing operation (the blob stays committed and unreferenced until it is referenced or expires), and between the promotion commit and the staging file's deletion;
- an orphan expiring;
- staging at its bound.

### 8.17 Data handling: provenance, allowlists, retention and locked behavior (privacy and retention)

- **Provenance:** every record carries its origin (source, capture, device, Core or migration), its authority (owner, model, source, system or ambiguous), source times, last retrieval or sync, revisions, interpretation status, and whether it is canonical, local or provisional.
- **Replication allowlist:**
  - **Clients receive:**
    - owner entities: tasks, ideas, captures, permitted preferences, owner-visible proposal fields, owner reminders, suggestions;
    - derived records: calendar occurrences in the window, briefs for 30 days, plans, bounded evidence excerpts, news cited by briefs or captures, source freshness;
    - trust and coordination: the public roster and artifacts, lineage, the backup catalogue, their own assignments, the trust bundle.
  - **Never replicated:** secrets and secret references, provider tokens and the token store; connector configuration and cursors; raw content and full email bodies; deliveries, the submission journal and audit; v1 parents (with R1 fingerprints) and idempotency records; the bodies of restricted legacy siblings, of which only redacted receipts replicate (§8.12.2); quarantined trust bundles; other devices' credentials or push tokens; Core's model settings.
- **Export allowlist:**
  - Core's owner export: owner entities, derived records, operation metadata, and waiting, rejected and quarantined work. Never secrets, verifiers or tokens.
  - Client archive: unsynchronized work only (§8.6.6).
- **Core retention:**
  - raw content 90 days with a 500 MB cap; delivery history 180 days; audit 365 days; unreferenced feed items 30 days — R1's defaults, §1.6;
  - change-log history 90 days;
  - operation metadata, tombstones (with request keys) and compact v1 parents for the life of the dataset;
  - operation bodies per §8.4.5; v1 response bodies per §8.12.2;
  - backups per the retained set R and owner pins;
  - the recovery ledger for the life of the dataset.
- **Client retention:** the working set; the journal per §8.6.4; blobs by reference count; staging dropped after installation or invalidation; bounded logs.
- **Deletion:** §8.6.7.
- **Locked devices:**
  - The replica and Keychain items are available after first unlock.
  - Before the first unlock after a restart, nothing is saved (§8.6.2).
  - Notification actions and Siri work while locked after first unlock; completing a task requires unlocking.
  - Background refresh may run locked; nearby runs only in the foreground.
  - Secure Enclave keys are usable after first unlock.
- **Diagnostics and logs:** IDs, counters, hashes and states only, with secrets and private content redacted (R2-API-04, CONSTRICTION).

### 8.18 Compatibility, fixtures, reproducibility and live-service limits (verification and maintainability)

- **Old clients:**
  - R1 companions use v1 through the translation layer until upgraded (§8.12.2, §8.14.5). Their actual behavior and presentation are documented from R1's code.
  - The R1 Mac app is replaced, not kept running (§8.14.4).
  - Legacy devices are never roster members.
  - An R2 client meeting an unknown required feature gets `upgrade_required`.
- **Frozen R1 artifacts, reproducible from a fresh checkout** (M0):
  1. Reconstruct R1's sources with `git show 491c90a:<path>`, walking the tree from `git show 491c90a:` recursively, into `.tmp/r2/<M0 run>/r1-src/`. This is permitted Git use (§3). A `git archive` or a checkout of another commit is not.
  2. Build there with Swift 6.3.2: R1's `lisa` (debug and release), plus a small frozen-client harness linking R1's `LisaClient` and offline queue.
  3. Generate synthetic R1 datasets and queue files with that build: pending, uncertain and dry-run deliveries; every queue state of §8.14.3; schedules and budgets.
  4. Record the SHA-256 of every source file, binary and fixture, and the commands, in `docs/VERIFICATION-SCENARIOS.md`.

  A later run can rebuild the same artifacts from the same commit. An R1 Apple build is the owner's step at X3 from the same tree (§8.14.4).
- **Scenario records:** each S-nn in `docs/VERIFICATION-SCENARIOS.md` names its commands, inputs, tool versions (Swift, SQLite, OpenSSL, engine), input hashes, expected outcomes and evidence location (the run directory under `.tmp/r2/`). Results are recorded only when a run produced them.
- **Live-service limits (owner decisions, 2026-11-22):** Claude never contacts a live mailbox, calendar, X, Discord, model provider, SMTP server, Twilio or APNs, and never uses the owner's Apple, Microsoft, Google, iCloud, Proton, OpenAI, Anthropic, Ollama or Twilio accounts, credentials, existing sessions or connected-account tools. Every credentialed or real-service check is the owner's, run under the owner's identity with the owner-verification harness (§10.8); until the owner runs it, it waits on the owner's checklist with its actual cause (§10.1). Claude verifies the production code against deterministic fixtures and controlled servers in the authorized rootless environment, including a controlled local Ollama server, which exercises Ollama's real protocol without any owner account; that result is reported apart from a run against the owner's deployment.

### 8.19 Required integrations: mail, calendars, models and calls (owner decision of 2026-11-22; MAIL-01, MAIL-02, CAL-01, CAL-02, EMAIL-01, CALL-01, MODEL-01–04, R2-INT-01–05, R2-NOT-06)

**Required (owner decision):** working production integrations, with comprehensive tests, for both mail and calendar on personal Outlook.com, personal Gmail with Google Calendar, iCloud Mail and iCloud Calendar, and Proton Mail and Proton Calendar — Microsoft 365 tenants and Google Workspace are outside the required matrix; OpenAI, Anthropic Claude and a self-hosted Ollama on the owner's machine or private network (not Ollama Cloud), both for Core inference and for the authorized Apple fallback; and R1's Twilio Programmable Voice. Every calendar is read through **authenticated, private, read-only** access: a link that anyone holding it can open is unacceptable, even for a synthetic test calendar, and a file import, a manual export, a simulated provider or an anonymously readable feed can't certify a live calendar integration. Required integrations stay in the release gate even when they are disabled by default or no credential is configured; at runtime each is enabled only by the owner's explicit opt-in.

#### 8.19.1 Accounts, authorization and credential custody

- **Dedicated Lisa accounts (Mechanism).** Each provider is reached through an account that exists only for Lisa. Its mailbox is Lisa's dedicated mailbox (MAIL-01), its address is the identity Lisa sends from (EMAIL-01), and the owner shares the calendars Lisa should read with it, **read-only**, from the owner's own account. No connector signs in to the owner's personal account. This keeps R2-SCOPE-02 ("Lisa does not obtain access to the owner's personal mailbox"; calendar subscriptions read-only) and `CONSTRICTION.md`'s rule against silently turning subscribed sources into account access. It also confines credentials that providers can't narrow — an iCloud app-specific password reaches all of an account's mail, contacts and calendars, and every IMAP scope is read-write — to Lisa's own account, while the share enforces read-only on the owner's calendars at the provider.
- **OAuth 2.0 for Microsoft and Google** (`LisaProviderAuth`):
  - one public client per provider, registered by the owner. Microsoft: an app registration in an Entra directory with "Personal Microsoft accounts only" and authority `consumers`. Google: a Desktop client in a Google Cloud project that **the dedicated Google account itself creates with user type Personal**, so no verification, user cap, unverified-app screen or 7-day token expiry applies. If Personal mode isn't available to that account, External with publishing status "In production" (unverified, a one-time warning screen) is the documented fallback; "Testing" is never used, because its tokens expire after 7 days;
  - authorization code with PKCE and a loopback redirect. `lisa core connector authorize <provider> <account>` prints the consent URL; the owner completes consent in any browser and either pastes the final redirected address back into the CLI, or lets it arrive through an SSH forward of the loopback port. The device-code flow isn't used: Google doesn't allow Gmail or Calendar scopes in it, and Microsoft's own documentation disagrees about personal accounts.
- **The provider-token store.** Refresh tokens rotate (Microsoft replaces its token on every use), so they can't live in read-only secret files. Core keeps them in `<data>/provider-tokens/`, one file per account, encrypted with AES-256-GCM under a key from the secret file `provider-token-key` (§8.11.2). A rotated token is written and `fsync`ed before the new access token is used. Tokens are Core-only secrets: never replicated, exported, logged or displayed; backups hold only ciphertext, which is useless without the separately kept key.
- **Failure and revocation.** `invalid_grant`, or a 401 after a refresh — revoked consent, a password change on an account with Gmail scopes, six months unused, Google's limit of 100 tokens per client and account, Microsoft's 90-day lifetime — puts the source in `needs_authorization`: its data is marked stale, the planner treats its calendars as unverified, nothing is deleted, and the owner sees "Authorize again: lisa core connector authorize …". `lisa core connector revoke <account>` deletes the local tokens and names the provider page where the owner removes the grant (Microsoft account › Privacy › Apps and services; Google Account › Third-party connections).
- **Passwords for iCloud and Proton:** an iCloud app-specific password (two-factor authentication required) and the Proton Mail Bridge password are ordinary Core secret files, as R1's IMAP and SMTP passwords are (§1.6).
- **Sender fences for OAuth channels:** rotating a provider mail channel means revoking the grant at the provider and authorizing again (§8.10.6).

#### 8.19.2 Mail and calendar, per provider

| Provider and service | Production route | Authentication and refresh | Supported scope | Owner prerequisites | Milestone | Differences from R1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Outlook.com mail | Microsoft Graph v1.0: `/me/mailFolders/{folder}/messages/delta` for new mail, `/messages/{id}/$value` for raw MIME, with immutable IDs; `/me/sendMail` for briefs and reminders | OAuth code with PKCE, `consumers`; the rotating refresh token in the token store | `Mail.Read` (read-only at the provider), `Mail.Send`, `offline_access` | A dedicated Outlook.com account; an app registration in an Entra directory (an Azure account or the Microsoft 365 Developer Program); consent once | M7 | R1's IMAP password login no longer works for Outlook.com; delta tokens and immutable IDs replace UIDs. IMAP with XOAUTH2 (`IMAP.AccessAsUser.All` is read-write; IMAP is off by default) isn't used |
| Outlook.com calendar | Graph `/me/calendars` (the shared calendar appears once the dedicated account accepts the share) and `/me/calendars/{id}/calendarView` over the window, paged by `@odata.nextLink`, times requested in UTC. Changes are found by comparing event IDs and `changeKey`, because v1.0 delta covers only the primary calendar | As above | `Calendars.Read`, `Calendars.Read.Shared` | The owner shares a calendar with "Can view all details"; the dedicated account accepts | M7 | Replaces the secret ICS link. **Unconfirmed:** Microsoft doesn't document reading, through Graph, a calendar one personal account shares with another (E-13) |
| Gmail | Gmail API: `users.messages.list` and `get` (`format=raw`), `users.history.list` for increments (404 → full rescan); `users.messages.send` for briefs and reminders | OAuth code with PKCE (Desktop client, Personal user type); the refresh token in the token store | `gmail.readonly` (read-only at the provider), `gmail.send` | A dedicated Gmail account that creates its own Cloud project and OAuth client | M7 | Gmail refuses R1's password login; IMAP with OAuth would need the full-access `https://mail.google.com/` scope, so the API is used; message and history IDs replace UIDs |
| Google Calendar | Calendar API v3 `events.list` per shared calendar ID with `syncToken` increments (410 → full resync), cancelled instances included. Masters, exceptions (`recurringEventId`, `originalStartTime`) and cancellations become iCalendar components expanded by R1's `LisaICalendar` | As above | `calendar.readonly`, which covers calendars shared with the account (`calendar.events.owned.readonly` doesn't) | The owner shares the calendar ("See all event details") with the dedicated account, which accepts it | M7 | Replaces the "secret address in iCal format" |
| iCloud Mail | IMAP `imap.mail.me.com:993` (TLS) through R1's `LisaIMAP` (`EXAMINE`, `BODY.PEEK[]` only); SMTP `smtp.mail.me.com:587` (STARTTLS) through `LisaSMTP` | An app-specific password, a Core secret; no refresh; revoked by any change of the account password | The password reaches the whole account, so read-only rests on Lisa's closed IMAP command set | A dedicated Apple Account with two-factor authentication and an app-specific password | M7 | None in protocol; configuration and limits documented |
| iCloud Calendar | CalDAV (RFC 4791) through `LisaCalDAV`: principal and calendar-home discovery from `https://caldav.icloud.com/`, `sync-collection` (RFC 6578) increments with an ETag fallback, `calendar-query` for the window. Only PROPFIND, REPORT and GET are ever sent; ICS is parsed by `LisaICalendar` | The same app-specific password | Read-only on the owner's calendar through a read-only share; Lisa sends no write method | The owner shares the calendar privately and read-only with the dedicated Apple Account, which accepts on iCloud.com | M7 | Replaces the public or secret `webcal` link. **Unconfirmed:** Apple documents app-specific passwords for third-party calendar apps but not the CalDAV endpoint, nor whether shared calendars appear to such apps (E-14) |
| Proton Mail | Proton Mail Bridge in a sidecar container that shares Core's network namespace, so its local-only IMAP (1143) and SMTP (1025) listeners are reachable at `127.0.0.1`; R1's `LisaIMAP` and `LisaSMTP`, with Bridge's own certificate pinned | The Bridge password, a Core secret; Bridge keeps the Proton session in its own password store | Lisa's closed IMAP command set; Bridge itself has full access to the account | A dedicated Proton account on a paid plan; the owner's one-time interactive Bridge login (two-factor) inside the sidecar; **P-09**, the owner's approval of the Bridge sidecar (§15) | M7, if P-09 is granted | No protocol change; a new runtime component. Bridge's Linux packages are 64-bit x86 only, so an arm64 Core host can't run it |
| Proton Calendar | **No compliant route.** Proton documents no API and no CalDAV ("Proton Calendar doesn't support CalDAV … The only option right now is to subscribe to a calendar via a shared link"), Bridge serves mail only, and a shared link is readable by anyone who holds it (a full-view link carries the decryption key) | — | — | — | — | The required row stays open with this evidence; changing the requirement is the owner's decision (§22) |

- **Incoming mail, on every provider:**
  - only the dedicated mailbox is ever authenticated;
  - **read-only:** Graph `Mail.Read` and `gmail.readonly` can't change flags; IMAP keeps R1's closed command set (`EXAMINE`, `UID SEARCH`, `BODY.PEEK[]`), so `\Seen` is never set (MAIL-01);
  - **cursors and pages:** Graph delta links and `@odata.nextLink`, Gmail history IDs and page tokens, IMAP UIDs with `UIDVALIDITY` (MAIL-02). A cursor commits in the connector's transaction together with what it ingested (§8.3.5), so a restart repeats nothing;
  - **changed identities:** a changed `UIDVALIDITY`, an expired Graph delta token or a Gmail history 404 starts a full rescan;
  - **duplicates:** a message is identified by its provider ID, its RFC 5322 `Message-ID` and a body digest, so a rescan ingests nothing twice;
  - **MIME and attachments:** raw MIME through R1's `LisaMIME`, with R1's size limits and unsupported types shown as unsupported;
  - **revocation:** `needs_authorization`; data kept; the owner told.
- **Outgoing mail** (R1's brief and reminder email, EMAIL-01): sent only from the dedicated account's own address and only to R1's approved addresses, with other recipients refused before any provider call; through the submission journal and its holds (§8.10.6). Provider limits are documented in each setup guide: Outlook.com's free-account limits are unpublished and lower than a subscriber's 5,000 recipients a day; Gmail allows 500 messages a day; iCloud 1,000 messages and 1,000 recipients a day; Proton's limit depends on the account's reputation, with at most 100 recipients a message.
- **Calendars, on every provider:**
  - private, authenticated access, refused after revocation: `needs_authorization`, the days shown "Using saved calendar information", never as free;
  - an initial sync, then incremental syncs;
  - zones and DST from each provider's zone data and R1's embedded zones;
  - recurring series with moved and cancelled instances; all-day and multi-day events by R1's overlap rule (§1.6); cancelled events removed;
  - **no write path at all:** no write method is implemented, and no write scope is requested;
  - provider changes picked up at the next poll; a stale or partial read never replaces a window (coverage per source, §8.4.2);
  - retries with backoff that honor `Retry-After`; conservative planning while a source is unverified (CAL-01's conservative behavior).

  Each provider's propagation delay is documented in its setup guide, and owner-live cases wait within a stated bound (15 minutes by default) before recording a failure with the cause "not propagated within the bound".
- **Optional public calendars:** R1's `LisaCalendarFeeds` stays for genuinely public calendars (holidays, for example). A secret or shared link on one of the four providers' hosts is refused at configuration, with a pointer to the authenticated connector.

#### 8.19.3 Model providers, on Core and as the Apple fallback

| Provider | Route | Structured output | Usage for budgets | Errors and uncertainty | Configuration example (placeholders) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| OpenAI | Chat Completions through `LisaOpenAICompatible`, extended: `response_format` with `json_schema` and `strict: true`; `max_completion_tokens`; sampling parameters only where the model's profile allows them; `X-Client-Request-Id` set to Lisa's request ID; `x-request-id` recorded | Strict schema; `message.refusal` and `finish_reason: "length"` count as invalid results | `prompt_tokens`, `completion_tokens` | 401 and 403 authorization; 404 unknown model; 429 rate limit (honoring `Retry-After`) or `insufficient_quota`; 5xx; a timeout after sending is `uncertain` and never resent, because no idempotency key exists | `provider: "openai"`, `endpoint: "https://api.openai.com/v1"`, `model: "<model ID>"`, `credential: "keychain:<item>"` on a device or `"file:<path>"` on Core |
| Anthropic | The Messages API through `LisaAnthropic`: `anthropic-version` pinned in configuration (`2026-10-21` when read), `system` at the top level, `max_tokens` sized to include thinking, no sampling parameters; content read by block type | `output_config.format` with `json_schema` (no recursive schemas, numeric or length constraints; Lisa's own validator still enforces every rule) | `input_tokens` plus both cache fields; `output_tokens`, thinking included | `stop_reason: "refusal"` (HTTP 200) and `"max_tokens"` count as invalid results; 401 and 403; 402 billing; 404 model; 413; 429 with `retry-after`, or the spend-limit 429 without it (failed, not retried); 500; 504 `timeout_error`; 529 `overloaded_error`; `request-id` recorded; uncertainty as above | `provider: "anthropic"`, `endpoint: "https://api.anthropic.com"`, `apiVersion: "2026-10-21"`, `model: "<model ID>"`, `credential: …` |
| Ollama (self-hosted) | Native `POST /api/chat` through `LisaOllama`, with `stream: false`, `options.num_ctx` set from Lisa's bounded context (the 4,096-token default would cut prompts silently), and `keep_alive` | `format` with the JSON schema, validated by Lisa | `prompt_eval_count`, `eval_count` | 404 model missing; 503 queue full (refused before processing); Ollama has no authentication of its own, so on a private network the owner puts it behind a TLS reverse proxy, which may require a bearer token that Lisa sends | `provider: "ollama"`, `endpoint: "http://127.0.0.1:11434"` (loopback) or `"https://10.0.0.5"` with a pinned SPKI (a private literal), `model: "<model name>"` |

- **Owner-run Ollama setup** (`Harness/docs/SETUP-OLLAMA.md`): install Ollama on the owner's machine or a private-network host and pull the chosen model; for the Mac on the same machine, keep it on `127.0.0.1:11434`; for Core and the iPhone and iPad, put a TLS reverse proxy with a bearer token in front of it on a private address, and pin the proxy's SPKI in each configuration. Lisa never exposes Ollama beyond what the owner configures.
- **Endpoint policy:** R1's model-endpoint rules stay (§1.6): endpoints named by DNS are public only (A records only on Apple, §8.9.3); `localhost`, loopback or private IP literals may use local networks; plain `http` only on loopback. Ollama's `/v1/chat/completions` endpoint also works through `LisaOpenAICompatible` as an alternative configuration, but isn't the default: it can't set the context size per request and has no strict mode.
- **Unsupported optional features are marked:** none of Lisa's workflows uses tools, streaming or images, so they are off for every provider; Anthropic's OpenAI compatibility layer isn't used, because Anthropic doesn't consider it production-ready.
- **Shared rules:** R2-INT-01–05 and §8.9.2 apply to every provider: device-local credentials and consent, one request in flight, ten a day, one repair, no resend after a possibly charged timeout, restarts without duplicate spending, withdrawal of consent, and the R2-INT-02 workflows with Core unavailable. Core inference uses the same adapters with Core's credentials (R2-TRUST-04).

#### 8.19.4 Calls (Twilio Programmable Voice)

- **Kept from R1 (CALL-01):** the Calls resource with TwiML `<Say>`; the approved number, urgency, acknowledgement, quiet-hour and rate controls (§1.6); and the submission journal and holds (§8.10.6).
- **Lifecycle evidence:** Core is reachable only over the VPN, so it can't receive Twilio's status callbacks. After an accepted create, it reads the call resource (`GET …/Calls/{Sid}.json`, read-only, with bounded backoff for up to 30 minutes) and records queued, ringing, in-progress, completed, busy, failed, no-answer or canceled. Twilio's acceptance, the call's lifecycle, its receipt by the phone and the spoken message are recorded as four separate facts.
- **No automatic second call:** Twilio documents no idempotency key for creating calls, so a timeout after submission, a restart or a restore leaves the call `outcomeUnknown` and held (§8.10.6); only the owner's release places another.
- **Account-free tests** run against a controlled Twilio-shaped server; Twilio's test credentials (which never place a real call) and the live call to the owner's designated test number are owner-run cases (§10.8).

**Acceptance (M7, M8; S-21, S-22, S-23; owner live sessions LS-1–LS-3):** the cases per provider, capability and environment are listed in §10.9.

## 9. Milestones (R2-DEV-01)

Every milestone ends with:
- Fedora debug and release builds and `swift format lint`;
- temporary verification in a fresh run directory;
- scenario records;
- the owner-harness parts the milestone owes (below), built from a clean checkout and run in fixture mode;
- file-by-file commits ending at a buildable checkpoint (§13);
- a checkpoint update and a short report.

**Dependencies on owner sessions:**
- Owner sessions follow the milestone shown. Fedora work never waits for them, except where a session result is a stated prerequisite: **N-1 gates the production nearby transport (M6), and E-1 gates the production retrieval design (M7).** Both are release requirements (§22).
- Components are introduced only when their module exists. M5 apps don't use fallback inference (M7) or notification ownership (M8) until those milestones add them.

**Container use:**
- **M2:** the first real use of the rootless engine, running the tmpfs exhaustion harness. It records the engine, context, architecture, image digests and results.
- **M4:** the first production image; its digest goes into the external build record.

**Firing identity across milestones.** The restore checks at M3 and M4 (S-11, N-A) need occurrence and firing identity, slot holds and reservations before the device side exists. `LisaFiringIdentity` and the Core side of ownership are therefore built at M3 and driven by a scripted device client. M8 adds the device side and repeats those scenarios with the real implementation.

**The owner-verification harness is delivered with the milestones** (§10.8). Claude builds each part; the owner runs its device and live cases. Every Apple runner and live-session guide ships at or before the milestone that precedes its session.

| Milestone | Buildable afterward | Proposed evidence | Owner-harness delivery (§10.8) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **M0 — Baseline** | The whole R1 package on Fedora; frozen R1 sources, binaries, client harness and fixtures (§8.18); `R2-CHECKPOINT.md` | The unmodified R1 suite in debug and release, outcomes recorded as they are; failures classified (§10.3); narrow production fixes, one per commit, then a rerun; `lisa demo`; hashes. R2-L01 (first), R2-L02 (baseline) | — |
| **M1 — Contracts and Apple proofs** | LisaSyncProtocol, LisaTrust, LisaCrypto additions, LisaArchive codec; `Apps/Proof` (syntax-checked) | Codec, size-derivation and token vectors; Merkle paging vectors; the proof-only certificate encoder checked with `openssl`. R2-L05 (codec), L16 (signatures, rollback), L32 (encoding). **X1:** N-1, E-1, E-2 | **Harness core:** `lisa-verify` with fixture, device and live modes, the result schema, reports and redacted export, case and run IDs, resume and cleanup, the Apple preflight; quick start and Apple setup guide. **X1 runner** (`Apps/OwnerVerification`, test plan `X1`). S-24 (fixture mode) |
| **M2 — Replication engine** | LisaMerge, LisaReplicaStore (with membership records and deferred pages), LisaLocalSearch, LisaSQLite and LisaTime additions; first container use | R2-L03, L06 (replica), L07, L09–L12, L15, L17, L18 (replica side), L33 (`max_page_count` and container `ENOSPC`), L35. S-06, S-07 (replica part), S-08 (replica part: deferral, constant-size publication, absence in both orders), S-17. Peak staging, index and WAL sizes | — |
| **M3 — Core, v1 and the direct HTTPS path** | Schema v5; LisaCoreLedger (release markers, interval coverage, SQLite-resident blobs), LisaRecoveryLedger (writer and sender fences with bound final positions, exported tails, journal `claimID`s and terminal checkpoints, trust bundles); LisaCoreAPI; **LisaLegacyRequests** (chained children, held suffixes, outcome-only parents, restricted siblings, error templates, terminal replies) and the v1 translation; **LisaFiringIdentity** and the Core side of notification ownership with a scripted device client; authority path; enrollment and trust lifecycle; restore, fencing, anchors and admission; LisaSyncEngine's Core session; **LisaSyncHTTP**; `lisa core`; `Apps/Probe` (syntax-checked) | End to end against the real TLS server. **The real Core incarnation and overflow tests** (L18 Core side, S-07 Core part). ## 10. Verification

### 10.1 Evidence classes, reported separately

| Class | Covers | Where |
|---|---|---|
| Fedora host | Builds, the R1 suite, R2 harnesses, the demo, host performance | This account; Fedora 45, x86_64, Swift 6.3.2 |
| Rootless container | Container harnesses (from M2), image builds and runs (from M4), controlled servers | The owner's rootless engine on this host |
| Target host | VPN and off-VPN reachability over IPv4 and IPv6, SELinux confinement, cutover | The owner's server, run by the owner |
| arm64 | The arm64 image | R1's historical run only (§1.4); R2 build and run `NOT_RUN_ENVIRONMENT` (Claude; missing platform) |
| Apple hardware | Builds and device checks | Owner sessions X1–X7, run by the owner with the owner harness |
| Simulated | In-memory peer link, simulated notification center, scripted providers, controlled provider-shaped servers | Labeled; never counted as another class |
| Controlled real-protocol server | A real server implementation run locally with no owner account: the controlled local Ollama container | Labeled; reported apart from the owner's own deployment |
| Owner live | Real provider accounts, real messages, notifications, model charges and calls, under the owner's identity | Owner live sessions LS-1–LS-3 and the live parts of X5 and X6; results the owner chooses to share, redacted |

**Statuses, executors and causes.** Every check carries one of R2's statuses, its **executor** (Claude or the owner) and its actual **cause**:
- `PASS` or `FAIL`, with the run's evidence location;
- `NOT_RUN_ENVIRONMENT` — a missing platform, hardware or software (named), an environment configuration, a provider capability that doesn't exist, or a pending physical or owner session;
- `NOT_RUN_CREDENTIALS` — an otherwise authorized run without the credentials or account access it needs. For an owner-run check, this means the owner hasn't yet provisioned the dedicated account, grant, key or profile it names;
- `BLOCKED_PERMISSION` — a required action by that executor lacks authorization: for example, implementing the Proton Bridge sidecar before P-09 is granted;
- `OUT_OF_SCOPE`.

Claude's lack of access to the owner's accounts is a standing boundary, not a pending item. No check requiring an owner account is ever assigned to Claude; each such check is the owner's and waits on the owner's checklist, with its own cause, until the owner runs it. Only a completed owner-run check supplies a live or physical result. Failed and unverified required rows, and their gates, stay open.

**Result levels for integrations and devices,** never merged:

| Result | Meaning | Executor |
|---|---|---|
| `harness-ready` | The case exists in the owner harness, builds from a clean checkout, and its fixture mode passes | Claude |
| `fixture-pass` | The production adapter passes against deterministic fixtures and controlled servers | Claude |
| `provider-accepted` | A real provider accepted the request in an owner-run case (for example SMTP 250, Graph 202, APNs 200, Twilio 201) | Owner |
| `owner-verified` | The owner observed the required outcome end to end: the email in the mailbox, the alert on the device, the call and its spoken message, the calendar state read correctly | Owner |

A required live row closes only at `owner-verified`, with every fixture and fault case of that row at `fixture-pass` (§10.9).

`docs/REQUIREMENTS-MATRIX.md` keeps an **Unavailable** list with each blocked check's executor and exact cause, and independent work continues.

### 10.2 Temporary harnesses and scenario records (O-6)

- Each run gets a fresh `.tmp/r2/<run-id>/`. It holds a separate SwiftPM package that depends on the root package by path, uses swift-testing, and calls public APIs only. Nothing in it is committed, overwritten or removed.
- Frozen R1 artifacts come from M0 (§8.18).
- `docs/VERIFICATION-SCENARIOS.md` is tracked. For every scenario it records commands, inputs, versions, hashes, expected outcomes and the evidence location of each run.
- The tracked owner-verification harness (§10.8) is a separate deliverable and never uses these temporary harnesses or their files.

| Scenario | Content | Milestones and sessions | Sections |
|---|---|---|---|

| S-02 | R1 queue classification with unresolved history and capture verdicts over every effect-bearing input (titles, shared URLs); post-downgrade reconciliation: the rollback cut first, task-create proof from R1's ledger or indeterminate, preference set, confirm and delete rules, overlapping exports, restarts | M9 | §8.14.3, §8.14.8 |
| S-03 | The one legacy converter: 50 valid changes and reverse-order relay; held administrative children in import-first and v1-first histories with `apply`, `refuse` and discard; outcome-only migrated parents; restricted siblings in actual relay bytes; Mac import with separate grants; mobile link; parts out of order; interruption; prefixes | M3 (v1 paths), M9 (imports), X7 | §8.12.2, §8.14.4, §8.14.5 |
| S-04 | Enrollment chain matrix with leaf pinning in both modes; zero enrollment POSTs and no token bytes on trust failure; the 7-day purge | M3, X2 | §8.1.2, §8.1.4 |
| S-05 | Credential retry storage | M3, X2 | §8.1.2 |
| S-06 | Full snapshot installation under writes; the backlog evaluated before applying; deferred pages and absence after the swap | M2 | §8.6.3 |
| S-07 | Pieces, value limits, paged resolution, reviewed-set artifacts across epochs and exports (operation-first and artifact-first), blob lists | M2 (replica), M3 (Core), M5 (exports) | §8.3.2, §8.3.3, §8.6.6 |
| S-08 | Interval coverage: multi-day events across window rolls; deferred pages during a repair; constant-size publication; absence in both orders; readers before, during and after; crash, epoch and window changes; stale relayed pages | M2, M3, M6 | §8.4.2, §8.4.3 |

| S-14 | Destination policy: A records only, AAAA never dialed, AAAA-only names deferred, NAT64 synthesis, dual-stack and split-VPN paths | M7, X1, X5 | §8.9.3 |
